#### Step 1: Reduction to power-saving estimates for twisted Möbius sums

Fix a finite-order Hecke character $\nu$ of $K$. Let $L_K(s, \nu)$ be the corresponding Hecke $L$-function. We extend $\nu$ by zero to ideals not coprime to its conductor. We will deduce Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> from a power-saving estimate for $\nu$-twisted Möbius sums.

<a id="eq:power-savings"></a>
Let $\mu$ denote the ideal Möbius function on $K$. All original ideal sums run over nonzero integral ideals. As in Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">3</a>, the ideals in these sums are understood to be prime to $2$, $3$, and the conductor of $\nu$. For a norm scale $D>0$, consider $$A_1(D)=\sum_{\mathfrak n}
   \mu(\mathfrak n)\nu(\mathfrak n)
   W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak n)/D),$$ where $W\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty);\mathbb C)$ is a smooth cutoff function. Thus $A_1(D)$ is a smoothed $\nu$-twisted Möbius sum over ideals of norm comparable to $D$, with roughly $D$ terms. We seek a power saving over this size: for a fixed $\delta>0$ and every $\varepsilon>0$, $$\label{eq:power-savings}
A_1(D)\ll_{\nu,W,\varepsilon}D^{1-\delta+\varepsilon} \text{ for every }W\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty);\mathbb C).$$ The implication from <a href="#eq:power-savings" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:power-savings">[eq:power-savings]</a> to a zero-free half-plane is the smoothed Hecke version of the classical relation between Möbius sums and zero-free regions. In the zeta-function case, the equivalence between the Riemann Hypothesis and the bound $\sum_{n\le x}\mu(n)\ll_\varepsilon x^{1/2+\varepsilon}$ is due to Littlewood <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Lit12](#ref-Lit12)]; see also <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[MM09](#ref-MM09), 1]. Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">3</a> gives the complete argument needed here.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Step 2: Embedding the sum in a family

<a id="eq:intro-family"></a>
In order to estimate $A_1(D)$, we embed it into a family of such sums, parametrized by $u\in\mathcal O_K$, by introducing a sextic twist. We call an element of $\mathcal O$ *primary* if it is congruent to $1$ modulo $3$. The ring $\mathcal O=\mathbb Z[\omega]$ is Euclidean, so it has unique factorization and every ideal is principal; for an ideal coprime to $3$, multiplying any generator by a unique unit gives $n\equiv1\pmod3$, since the six units represent the six invertible residue classes modulo $3$. For a primary element $n$, we write $\mu(n)=\mu((n))$ and $\nu(n)=\nu((n))$; divisor sums count each ideal divisor once, using its primary generator. For an ideal $\mathfrak n$ prime to $6$, use its unique primary generator $n$ and write $$\chi_{\mathfrak n}(u)=\chi_n(u)=(u/n)_6.$$ Here $(u/n)_6$ is the sextic residue symbol, extended by zero when $(u,n)\neq 1$.[^1] We also write $(u/n)_2$ and $(u/n)_3$ for the quadratic and cubic residue symbols, defined by the same convention with $6$ replaced by $2$ and $3$, respectively. When $(n,6)=1$, they equal $\chi_n(u)^3$ and $\chi_n(u)^2$. Restrictions that keep these symbols defined are understood when omitted in the outline. The family is $$\label{eq:intro-family}
 A_u(D)=\sum_{\mathfrak n}
   \mu(\mathfrak n)\nu(\mathfrak n)\chi_{\mathfrak n}(u)
   W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak n)/D).$$

<a id="eq:intro-ms"></a>
Fix $0<\vartheta\le1/10$. The crucial estimate, Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a>, is that $$\label{eq:intro-ms}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)\le H}|A_u(D)|^2
       \ll_{\nu,W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}D^{1+\varepsilon}H,
 \qquad H=D^{1+\vartheta}.$$ In a mean square, we call the variable in the outer sum the *row* and the variable in the inner sum the *column*. In <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a> these are $u$ and $\mathfrak n$, respectively. Up to the factor of $D^\varepsilon$, <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a> can be thought of as saying that the family $\{A_u(D)\}$ exhibits “square-root cancellation” on average (in the $L^2$ sense) over $u$.

<a id="eq:A_u-vs-A_1"></a>
The mean-square estimate <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a> gives the desired power saving for $A_1(D)$ because $A_{p^6}(D)\approx A_1(D)$ for many primes $p$. For a primary prime $p$ with $Y/2<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)\le Y$, the identity $\chi_n(p^6)=\mathbf 1_{p\nmid n}$ leaves only $O_W(D/Y)$ differing terms and hence gives $$\label{eq:A_u-vs-A_1}
 A_{p^6}(D)=A_1(D)+O_W(D/Y).$$ By Landau’s prime ideal theorem (see <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), Theorem 5.33]), there are $\asymp Y/\log Y$ choices of such $p$. Taking $Y=H^{1/6}$, considering the contribution of such terms to <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a> and using <a href="#eq:A_u-vs-A_1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:A_u-vs-A_1">[eq:A_u-vs-A_1]</a> gives <a id="eq:intro-extraction"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-extraction}
|A_1(D)|^2
\ll D^{1+\varepsilon}H^{5/6}+D^2H^{-1/3}.$$ With $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$, this gives $A_1(D)\ll D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\varepsilon}$ for every fixed $0<\vartheta\le1/10$. Choosing $\vartheta$ sufficiently small for each requested exponent loss yields $A_1(D)\ll_{\nu,W,\varepsilon}D^{11/12+\varepsilon}$. Therefore it remains to establish <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Step 3: Poisson summation

We turn to the task of proving the mean-square estimate <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a>. Now that we have introduced the row variable $u$, we can apply Poisson summation in this variable. This introduces sextic Gauss sums, arising from the Fourier transforms of the sextic characters. Upon application of the Gauss–Jacobi identities, these sextic Gauss sums absorb the factor of $\mu$ and turn into cubic Gauss sums. We interpret the resulting cubic Gauss sums as coefficients of Kubota’s cubic theta function. In the next step, we use the automorphy of this theta function. For now, we explain the appearance of these Gauss sums in more detail.

<a id="eq:intro-gauss-sum"></a>
Define the additive character $$e(z)=\exp(4\pi\mathrm i\,\operatorname{Im}z/\sqrt3)\quad(z\in\mathbb C).$$ For a squarefree Eisenstein integer $n\equiv1\pmod3$ prime to $6$ and an integer $j$, define the normalized Gauss sums $$\label{eq:intro-gauss-sum}
 \gamma_j(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)}}
       \sum_{x\bmod n}\chi_n(x)^j e(x/n).$$ For $j=-1$, we interpret $\chi_n^{-1}$ as the conjugate character $\overline{\chi_n}$.

Consider the classical identity $$\left(\frac{-1}{m}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{x\bmod m}\left(\frac{x}{m}\right)e^{2\pi ix/m}\right)^2,$$ where $m$ is an odd squarefree positive integer and the symbols are Jacobi symbols. We would like a similar decomposition for $\mu$ instead of $\left(\frac{-1}{m}\right)$. Following the work of Hasse <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Has50](#ref-Has50), pp. 443–445] and Heath-Brown <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[HB00](#ref-HB00), (2)], we derive the following identity in Appendix <a href="#app:gauss-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:gauss-identities">8.1</a>, valid for squarefree primary $n$ away from a fixed set of excluded primes: $$\mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n),
 \qquad \alpha(n)=\frac{n}{|n|},\quad G(n)=\overline{\chi_n(4)}\gamma_3(n).$$ Here $G(n)$ is a fixed ray-class factor. The factor $\gamma_{-1}(n)$ comes from Poisson summation; as promised, it combines with $\mu(n)$ to produce the cubic coefficient $\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)$ (up to ray-class factors). Further details are given in Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">4</a>.

<a id="eq:intro-gauss-family"></a>
We call the sums obtained after applying Poisson summation *dual sums*.[^2] Rearranging these dual sums reduces the problem of estimating $\sum_u |A_u(D)|^2$ to estimating the following family of column sums (for clarity, we have suppressed auxiliary twists and coprimality conditions): $$\label{eq:intro-gauss-family}
 B_h(X)=\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal O\\n\equiv1\ (3)\\n\ {\rm squarefree}}}
       \overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\xi(n)\chi_n(h)
       U(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X).$$ Here $h$ is the new row variable, Fourier dual to $u$, while $U$ is a smooth weight restricting the column variable $n$ to norm comparable to $X$. We choose $\xi$ to be either $\nu\eta$ or $\overline{\nu}\eta$, where $\eta$ ranges over a finite set of ray class characters of fixed modulus.[^3] For each choice of $\xi$, this defines a family of sums $B_h(X)$.

<a id="eq:intro-poisson-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-dual-ms"></a>
After treating the diagonal and separating the smooth weights, we roughly get that $$\label{eq:intro-poisson-comparison}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)\le H}|A_u(D)|^2
 \preccurlyeq DH+\frac HD
       \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|B_h(D)|^2.$$ Here $\mathcal H\asymp D^2/H$ is the dual norm scale. This schematic comparison suppresses the common factors arising when the square is expanded. Thus the desired estimate is $$\label{eq:intro-dual-ms}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|B_h(D)|^2\preccurlyeq D^2.$$

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Step 4: Relation to cubic Gauss sums and Kubota’s cubic theta function

To estimate <a href="#eq:intro-dual-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-dual-ms">[eq:intro-dual-ms]</a>, we first identify the cubic Gauss coefficients with Fourier coefficients of a theta function. Its transformation law applies to a sum with extra cube factors, which we will remove in Step 5.

Let $\theta(z,v)$ be Kubota’s cubic theta function on hyperbolic three-space ($z\in\mathbb C$, $v \in \mathbb R_{>0}$), obtained as a residue of a cubic metaplectic Eisenstein series <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Kub69](#ref-Kub69), [Pat77](#ref-Pat77)]. We use the normalization of <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[DR](#ref-DR), 5.1, (5.6)–(5.8)], recalled below in <a href="#eq:cubic-theta-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-theta-definition">[eq:cubic-theta-definition]</a>.

<a id="eq:intro-theta-coefficients"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-theta-family"></a>
Put $\lambda=1+2\omega$. For primary elements $n,b\in\mathcal O=\mathbb Z[\omega]$, with $n$ squarefree and $(nb,6)=1$, let $c_\theta(nb^3)$ denote the Fourier coefficient of $\overline\theta$ indexed by $\lambda^{-3}nb^3$ in its expansion in $z$. Patterson’s formula <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Pat77](#ref-Pat77), Theorem 8.1], recorded in <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[DR](#ref-DR), (5.7)], gives $$\label{eq:intro-theta-coefficients}
 c_\theta(nb^3)
     =3^{5/2}|b|\,\overline{\chi_n(\lambda)^2}\gamma_2(n).$$ Taking $b=1$ in <a href="#eq:intro-theta-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-coefficients">[eq:intro-theta-coefficients]</a> and substituting into <a href="#eq:intro-gauss-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-gauss-family">[eq:intro-gauss-family]</a> yields $$\label{eq:intro-theta-family}
 B_h(X)=3^{-5/2}\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal O\\n\equiv1\ (3)\\n\ {\rm squarefree}}}
 c_\theta(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\chi_n(\lambda)^2
 \xi(n)\chi_n(h)U(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X).$$ Thus <a href="#eq:intro-theta-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-family">[eq:intro-theta-family]</a> expresses $B_h(X)$ as a smoothed, twisted sum of the squarefree-index coefficients of $\overline\theta$. This connection was used by Heath-Brown and Patterson to study Kummer sums <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[HBP79](#ref-HBP79)].

<a id="eq:intro-completed-theta-sum"></a>
To use the summation formula for theta coefficients, we embed the sum <a href="#eq:intro-theta-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-family">[eq:intro-theta-family]</a> in a completed sum, by including terms indexed by $nb^3$. This use of cube completion follows Dunn and Radziwiłł , with the underlying theta coefficients given by Patterson <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Pat77](#ref-Pat77), Theorem 8.1]. For fixed $h$, $\xi$, and $U$, define $$\label{eq:intro-completed-theta-sum}
T_h(X):=\frac{1}{3^{5/2}\sqrt X}
 \sum_{\substack{n,b\in\mathcal O\\n,b\equiv1\ (3)\\n\ {\rm squarefree}}}
 c_\theta(nb^3)\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}\chi_{nb^3}(\lambda)^2
 \xi(nb^3)\chi_{nb^3}(h)U(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(nb^3)/X).$$ The $b=1$ terms are exactly $X^{-1/2}B_h(X)$; the other terms supply the cube indices in the theta expansion. The first goal is to bound the mean square of $T_h(X)$ over $h$.

<a id="eq:intro-dual-theta-sum"></a>
The automorphy of $\theta$ gives a summation formula that transforms the completed column sum $T_h(X)$, with the row $h$ held fixed, into dual sums of theta coefficients with new smooth weights and character twists. A cusp is represented by a boundary point in $K\cup\{\infty\}$. A *cusp expansion* is the Fourier expansion in the horizontal variable after a change of coordinates taking infinity to that point. We call its Fourier coefficients *cusp coefficients*. Our starting point is a variant (established in Appendix <a href="#app:fixed-ray" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:fixed-ray">8.2</a>) of the theta transformation of Dunn and Radziwiłł , extending work of Patterson <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Pat77](#ref-Pat77)] and Yoshimoto <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Yos87](#ref-Yos87)]. The key point is how the character twists change. Suppose for illustration that $h$ is squarefree and primary, with $(h,6)=1$. Then, by Proposition <a href="#lem:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection">14</a>, the transformed expression is a finite linear combination of sums of the form $$\label{eq:intro-dual-theta-sum}
 \sum_{0\ne m\in\mathcal O}
 \frac{d_\theta(m)\alpha(m)}{\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(m)}}\,
 \chi_h(m)^3
 V_*^\sharp\!\Bigl(\frac{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(m)X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)^2}\Bigr).$$ The dual column index is $m$, while $h$ remains the row index. Here $d_\theta(m)$ denotes the coefficient at the Fourier index $\lambda^{-4}m$ in a cusp expansion of $\overline\theta$, and $V_*^\sharp$ is the transform in <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a>, applied to $V_*(y)=y^{1/2}U(y)$. We have suppressed a finite sum over these cusp expansions, fixed periodic twists, bounded prefactors, and fixed scale constants. Within each fixed ray class of $h$, the coefficient sequences and transformed weights are independent of $h$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:reflection-uniformity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection-uniformity">15</a>. Proposition <a href="#lem:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection">14</a> gives the precise formula, writing $d(\ell)$ for the cusp coefficient at $\ell=\lambda^{-4}m$.

Since $V_*^\sharp$ decays rapidly at infinity, the effective norm range in <a href="#eq:intro-dual-theta-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-dual-theta-sum">[eq:intro-dual-theta-sum]</a> is $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(m)\ll\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)^2/X$, in place of the original range $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(nb^3)\asymp X$. These dual sums arise from the theta transformation and involve theta coefficients on the transformed scale, now twisted by the quadratic character $\chi_h^3$. This character arises because, at each prime $p\mid h$, the Fourier and theta factors combine as <a id="eq:intro-quadratic-twist"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-quadratic-twist}
 \chi_p^{-1}\chi_p^{-2}=\chi_p^{-3}=\chi_p^3.$$ Now a key point is that since $\chi_p^3$ is quadratic, Goldmakher and Louvel’s quadratic large sieve (a generalization of Heath-Brown’s quadratic large sieve to number fields) bounds the mean square of the sums in <a href="#eq:intro-dual-theta-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-dual-theta-sum">[eq:intro-dual-theta-sum]</a> as $h$ varies. For squarefree quadratic families with row and column norm ranges $M,L$, the quadratic large sieve gives the factor $M+L$, up to $(ML)^\varepsilon$. Crucially, this avoids the additional term $(ML)^{2/3}$ in Blomer, Goldmakher, and Louvel’s general higher-order large sieve <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[BGL](#ref-BGL), Theorem 1.3].

<a id="eq:intro-completed-ms"></a>
We then apply Cauchy–Schwarz in the cube variable and the quadratic large sieve in the squarefree column variable to estimate the mean square of $T_h(X)$. The details are given in the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:R" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:R">10</a>, which gives, for $\mathcal H,X\ge1$, $$\label{eq:intro-completed-ms}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|T_h(X)|^2
 \ll_{\varepsilon,\xi,U}(\mathcal H X)^\varepsilon
       \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2}{X}\Bigr).$$ At $X=D$ and $\mathcal H\le D$, the heuristic comparison $B_h(D)\approx\sqrt D\,T_h(D)$ would therefore give the desired $D^2$ bound. The remaining task is to justify the corresponding mean-square bound by removing the cube factors.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Step 5: Removing the cube factors

We now pass from a mean-square bound for $T_h(X)$ to one for $X^{-1/2}B_h(X)$, with both averages taken over $h$. Note that one cannot simply discard the terms with $b\ne1$, because the contributions from different $b$ can cancel. We begin by undoing the addition of cube factors using Möbius inversion. Related completions appear in Patterson <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Pat77](#ref-Pat77), Theorem 6.1] and Heath-Brown <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[HB00](#ref-HB00), 3], with explicit removal of the cube factors by Möbius inversion in Dunn and Radziwiłł .

<a id="eq:intro-cube-completion"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-cube-weight"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-cube-inversion"></a>
Fix $h$ and $\xi$, and write $P_h(X)=X^{-1/2}B_h(X)$ for the $b=1$ part of $T_h(X)$. Substituting the coefficient formula <a href="#eq:intro-theta-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-coefficients">[eq:intro-theta-coefficients]</a> into <a href="#eq:intro-completed-theta-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-completed-theta-sum">[eq:intro-completed-theta-sum]</a> gives $$\label{eq:intro-cube-completion}
 T_h(X)=\sum_{\substack{b\in\mathcal O\\b\equiv1\ (3)}}\frac{w_h(b)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)}\,
                  P_h\!\Bigl(\frac{X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3}\Bigr),$$ where $$\label{eq:intro-cube-weight}
 w_h(b)=\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\xi(b)^3\chi_b(h)^3.$$ Each factor in <a href="#eq:intro-cube-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-weight">[eq:intro-cube-weight]</a> is completely multiplicative in $b$. Thus $w_h(bc)=w_h(b)w_h(c)$ even when $b,c$ share prime factors, and $|w_h(b)|\le1$. Möbius inversion (cf. <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), 1.3]) gives $$\label{eq:intro-cube-inversion}
 P_h(X)=\sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal O\\d\equiv1\ (3)}}\frac{\mu(d)w_h(d)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)}\,
                  T_h\!\Bigl(\frac{X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)^3}\Bigr).$$ Indeed, substituting <a href="#eq:intro-cube-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-completion">[eq:intro-cube-completion]</a> into <a href="#eq:intro-cube-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-inversion">[eq:intro-cube-inversion]</a> and grouping by the total cube index $b$ gives the factor $w_h(b)\sum_{d\mid b}\mu(d)$, which is $1$ for $b=1$ and $0$ otherwise.

The objective is now an estimate for $P_h$, rather than for the completed sum $T_h$. For the simplified family and the parameter ranges arising from Step 3, the required bound is

<a id="eq:intro-cube-target"></a>
$$\label{eq:intro-cube-target}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_h(X)|^2
       \ll_{\varepsilon,\xi,U}(\mathcal H X)^\varepsilon X.$$ Since $B_h(X)=\sqrt X\,P_h(X)$, this is equivalent to a bound of size $X^2$, up to the same small power, for the mean square of $B_h(X)$.

<a id="eq:intro-short-cube-ms"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-cube-cutoff"></a>
For $1\le H_c\le X^{1/3}$, let $P_{h,\le H_c}(X)$ be the part of <a href="#eq:intro-cube-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-inversion">[eq:intro-cube-inversion]</a> with $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\le H_c$. Applying weighted Cauchy–Schwarz for each fixed $h$, then summing over $h$ and using <a href="#eq:intro-completed-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-completed-ms">[eq:intro-completed-ms]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_{h,\le H_c}(X)|^2\\
 &\quad\le\Bigl(\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(e)\le H_c}\frac1{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(e)}\Bigr)
       \Bigl(\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\le H_c}\frac1{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)}
       \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}
       |T_h\!\bigl(X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)^3\bigr)|^2\Bigr)\\
 &\quad\preccurlyeq\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\le H_c}\frac1{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)}
       \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)^3}{X}\Bigr)
       \preccurlyeq\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2H_c^3}{X}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus $$\label{eq:intro-short-cube-ms}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_{h,\le H_c}(X)|^2
 \ll_{\varepsilon,\xi,U}(\mathcal H X)^\varepsilon
       \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2H_c^3}{X}\Bigr).$$ For $\mathcal H\le X$, the estimate <a href="#eq:intro-short-cube-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-short-cube-ms">[eq:intro-short-cube-ms]</a> is within the target <a href="#eq:intro-cube-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-target">[eq:intro-cube-target]</a> provided $\mathcal H^2H_c^3/X\le X$. Thus we apply the completed mean-square bound directly only up to the cutoff $$\label{eq:intro-cube-cutoff}
 H_c=\min\!\biggl\{X^{1/3},
                \Bigl(\frac{X}{\mathcal H}\Bigr)^{2/3}\biggr\}.$$ At the basic scales from Step 3, $$X\asymp D,\qquad \mathcal H\asymp D^{1-\vartheta},
 \qquad H_c\asymp D^{2\vartheta/3}.$$ The inverse sum can extend to $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\asymp D^{1/3}$, so we must still control the larger divisors.

<a id="eq:intro-long-cube-ms"></a>
<a id="eq:intro-energy-target"></a>
Write $\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)$ for the number of nonzero integral ideal divisors of $(b)$. Let $P_{h,>H_c}(X)$ denote the terms with $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)>H_c$ in <a href="#eq:intro-cube-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-inversion">[eq:intro-cube-inversion]</a>, and put $L_b=X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3$. Substituting <a href="#eq:intro-cube-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-completion">[eq:intro-cube-completion]</a> into the truncated inversion formula for $P_{h,>H_c}(X)$ obtained from <a href="#eq:intro-cube-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-inversion">[eq:intro-cube-inversion]</a>, and grouping by $b=dc$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 P_{h,>H_c}(X)
 &=\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)>H_c}\sum_c
   \frac{\mu(d)w_h(d)w_h(c)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(c)}
   P_h\!\Bigl(\frac{X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(dc)^3}\Bigr)\\
 &=\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)>H_c}
   \frac{\beta_0(b)\chi_b(h)^3}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)}P_h(L_b),
 \end{aligned}$$ where all indices are primary and $$\beta_0(b)=\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\xi(b)^3
       \sum_{\substack{d\mid b\\\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)>H_c}}\mu(d),
 \qquad |\beta_0(b)|\le\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b).$$ The support of $U$ restricts $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)\ll_U X^{1/3}$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz for each $h$, followed by summation, gives $$\label{eq:intro-long-cube-ms}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_{h,>H_c}(X)|^2
 &\le\Bigl(\sum_b\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)}\Bigr)
 \sum_b\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_h(L_b)|^2\\
 &\preccurlyeq\sup_b\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_h(L_b)|^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Put $a_\xi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\xi(n)$. Write $$E(\mathcal H,X)
 :=\frac1X\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}
 \Bigl|\sum_n^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(h)U(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X)\Bigr|^2.$$ A star restricts an index to squarefree primary elements. By <a href="#eq:intro-gauss-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-gauss-family">[eq:intro-gauss-family]</a> and the definition of $P_h$, we have $$E(\mathcal H,X)
 =\frac1X\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|B_h(X)|^2
 =\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)\ll\mathcal H}|P_h(X)|^2.$$ At the scales from Step 3, our goal is to prove $$\label{eq:intro-energy-target}
 E(\mathcal H,X)\preccurlyeq X.$$ At $X=D$, this is precisely the bound <a href="#eq:intro-dual-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-dual-ms">[eq:intro-dual-ms]</a> for the mean square of $B_h(D)$. Substitution into the Poisson comparison <a href="#eq:intro-poisson-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-poisson-comparison">[eq:intro-poisson-comparison]</a> then gives the required original mean-square estimate <a href="#eq:intro-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-ms">[eq:intro-ms]</a>. Combining <a href="#eq:intro-short-cube-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-short-cube-ms">[eq:intro-short-cube-ms]</a> for $P_{h,\le H_c}$ (the terms with $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\le H_c$) and <a href="#eq:intro-long-cube-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-long-cube-ms">[eq:intro-long-cube-ms]</a> for $P_{h,>H_c}$ (the terms with $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)>H_c$), with the cutoff <a href="#eq:intro-cube-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-cutoff">[eq:intro-cube-cutoff]</a>, gives $$E(\mathcal H,X)\preccurlyeq X + \sup_{b:\,1\le L_b\le X/H_c^3}E(\mathcal H,L_b).$$ It therefore remains to prove $E(\mathcal H,L_b)\preccurlyeq X$ for $1<L_b\le X/H_c^3$. Here the row range $\mathcal H$ stays fixed, and the required bound is still of size $X$ even though the column scale has decreased to $L_b$.

We now expand the square and apply Poisson summation in $h$ as before; we now record the proof in terms of $E(\mathcal H,L_b)$. The Gauss-sum coefficients become Möbius coefficients, and the new row variable $y$ has norm at most about $L_b^2/\mathcal H$. After separating the weights, this gives schematically $$\begin{aligned}
 E(\mathcal H,L_b)
 &\preccurlyeq X+\frac{\mathcal H}{L_b^2}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(y)\ll L_b^2/\mathcal H}|M(y)|^2,\\
 M(y)&=\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)\asymp L_b}^*
 \mu(n)\xi_1(n)\overline{\chi_n(y)}U_1(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/L_b).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $\xi_1$ is another fixed ray class character and $U_1$ is a smooth compactly supported weight produced by separating the variables. The term $X$ includes the diagonal and zero-frequency contributions, using $\mathcal H\le X$.

Since $L_b=X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3\le X$, we may enlarge the nonnegative sum over $y$ to $$\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(y)\ll Y,\qquad
 Y=\frac{XL_b}{\mathcal H}\ge\frac{L_b^2}{\mathcal H}.$$ The purpose of this enlargement is that a second application of Poisson summation gives a shorter row range: $$\mathcal H'=\frac{L_b^2}{Y}=\frac{\mathcal HL_b}{X}.$$ The coefficients return to cubic Gauss-sum coefficients, and $$\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(y)\ll Y}|M(y)|^2
 \preccurlyeq YL_b+Y E'(\mathcal H',L_b).$$ Here $E'$ has the same form as $E$, with possibly different smooth weights and fixed characters; $YL_b$ accounts for the zero-frequency contribution. Consequently, $$E(\mathcal H,L_b)
 \preccurlyeq X+\frac X{L_b} E'\!\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal HL_b}{X},L_b\Bigr),
 \qquad
 \frac{E(\mathcal H,L_b)}X
 \preccurlyeq 1+\frac{E'(\mathcal HL_b/X,L_b)}{L_b}.$$ Thus it suffices to prove $$E'\!\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal HL_b}{X},L_b\Bigr)\preccurlyeq L_b.$$ This is the original type of estimate at smaller parameters: $$(\mathcal H',X')
 =\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal H}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3},
         \frac{X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3}\Bigr),
 \qquad
 \frac{\mathcal H'}{X'}=\frac{\mathcal H}{X}.$$ Both scales decrease while their ratio stays fixed. The factor $X/L_b$ in the preceding inequality is exactly what converts the new target bound $L_b$ into the required bound $X$. These two Poisson summations give the *transfer estimate*: a bound for the remaining mean square in terms of new mean squares of the same type. For related uses of an enlarged summation range, see Goldmakher–Louvel <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[GL](#ref-GL), Lemma 4.4 and the proof of Theorem 4.1], following Heath-Brown <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[HB95](#ref-HB95), Lemma 9].

Combining the bounds <a href="#eq:intro-short-cube-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-short-cube-ms">[eq:intro-short-cube-ms]</a> and <a href="#eq:intro-long-cube-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-long-cube-ms">[eq:intro-long-cube-ms]</a> from the first part of Step 5 with the transfer estimate above gives the recursive bound $$\frac{E(\mathcal H,X)}{X}
 \preccurlyeq 1+\sup_{\substack{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)>H_c\\L_b>1}}\frac{E'(\mathcal H',X')}{X'},
 \qquad X'=L_b,\quad \mathcal H'=\frac{\mathcal H L_b}{X}.$$ For each $b$ in this supremum, the cutoff <a href="#eq:intro-cube-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cube-cutoff">[eq:intro-cube-cutoff]</a> gives $\mathcal H'<\mathcal H(\mathcal H/X)^2
\ll D^{-2\vartheta}\mathcal H$. Since $\mathcal H'/X'=\mathcal H/X$, the same contraction applies at every step. After $O_\vartheta(1)$ steps, every resulting mean square either has an empty remainder or has row parameter at most $1$, where counting gives the desired bound at its reduced scales. Applying the recursive inequality back through these steps proves <a href="#eq:intro-energy-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-energy-target">[eq:intro-energy-target]</a> for the original $E(\mathcal H,X)$, completing the sketch of the proof.

The preceding sketch suppresses auxiliary twists and common factors for the purpose of illustration. To carry out this argument with the auxiliary twists included, we use the family $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F)
 =\frac1{XF}\sum_{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(f)\asymp F}^*
   \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)\ll\mathcal H}
 \Bigl|\sum_n^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
                      U(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X)\Bigr|^2$$ and prove $\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F)\preccurlyeq XF$ in the parameter ranges of Proposition <a href="#prop:canonical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:canonical">9</a>. Unlike the sketch above, the full argument must also handle the common factors and the resulting dyadic ranges. The precise family is defined in <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a>, and Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">12</a> states the transfer estimate. Combining it with the bounds for the two parts of the inverse sum in Step 5 reduces the row range at each step. Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">5</a> proves the desired bound by a finite iteration, following the admissible-exponent method of Heath-Brown <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[HB95](#ref-HB95), Lemma 8 and 8], <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[HB00](#ref-HB00), Lemma 9]; see also <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[GL](#ref-GL), Theorem 4.1] and <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[BGL](#ref-BGL), 3.2].

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### From the mean-square estimate to the zero-free region

We first carry out Steps 1 and 2 of the outline: extract cancellation in $A_1(D)$ from a mean-square estimate for the family, then use a Mellin transform to deduce nonvanishing. Fix a finite-order Hecke character $\nu$ of $K$ and a finite set $S$ of prime ideals that contains all prime ideals above $2$ or $3$, as well as all prime ideals dividing the conductor of $\nu$. For an ideal or element $a$, write $(a,S)=1$ if no prime ideal in $S$ divides $a$. An ideal is supported on $S$ if all its prime factors belong to $S$. For $W\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty);\mathbb C)$, recall the family $$A_u(D):=\sum_{(n,S)=1}\mu(n)\nu(n)\chi_n(u)W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/D),$$ introduced in <a href="#eq:intro-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-family">[eq:intro-family]</a>. Here and throughout the original family, $n$ runs over ideals prime to $S$, represented by their primary generators. The key estimate is the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-ms"></a>
<div id="thm:ms" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:ms"></a>
**Proposition 3**. *For every fixed $0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there exists an integer $k=k(\vartheta,\varepsilon)\ge1$ such that, for every compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$, all smooth $W$ supported in $I$, and $D\ge2$, $$\label{eq:ms}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)\le D^{1+\vartheta}}|A_u(D)|^2
       \ll_{\nu,S,I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
       \Bigl(\max_{0\le j\le k}\|W^{(j)}\|_\infty\Bigr)^2
       D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}.$$*

</div>

We first deduce Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> assuming Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a>. The proof of the proposition is completed in Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">5.6</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-ms"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-ms</summary>

<a id="eq:mobius-saving"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> from Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a>.* Fix $0<\vartheta\le1/10$, and put $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$ and $Y=H^{1/6}=D^{(1+\vartheta)/6}$. For prime ideals $Y/2<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak p)\le Y$, $\mathfrak p\notin S$, let $p$ be their primary generators, chosen with $p\equiv1\pmod3$. Then $\chi_n(p^6)=\mathbf 1_{\mathfrak p\nmid n}$ and therefore $$|A_1(D)-A_{p^6}(D)|
 \le\|W\|_\infty
       \#\{n:(n,S)=1,\ \mathfrak p\mid n,\ \mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)\asymp D\}
 \ll_W D/Y.$$ For this fixed field, Landau’s prime ideal theorem (see <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), Theorem 5.33]) gives $J\asymp Y/\log Y$ such primes. Their sixth powers are distinct rows of norm at most $H$. Apply <a href="#eq:ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ms">[eq:ms]</a> with loss $\varepsilon/2$ and use $\log Y\ll_\varepsilon D^{\varepsilon/2}$ to obtain <a id="eq:prime-extract"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |A_1(D)|^2
 &\le \frac2J\sum_{\substack{Y/2<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)\le Y\\(p,S)=1}}|A_{p^6}(D)|^2+O_W(D^2/Y^2)\nonumber\\
 &\ll_{\nu,S,W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}/Y+D^2/Y^2
   =D^{11/6+5\vartheta/6+\varepsilon}+D^{5/3-\vartheta/3}.
                                                        \label{eq:prime-extract}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $A_1(D)\ll_{\nu,S,W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\varepsilon}$. Given any requested exponent loss, choose $\vartheta>0$ and then the loss in Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a> sufficiently small. Renaming the resulting loss $\varepsilon$, we obtain $$\label{eq:mobius-saving}
                 A_1(D)\ll_{\nu,S,W,\varepsilon}D^{11/12+\varepsilon}.$$

We now use <a href="#eq:mobius-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mobius-saving">[eq:mobius-saving]</a> to rule out a zero of $L_K(s,\nu)$ in $\operatorname{Re}s>11/12$. Suppose such a zero $\varrho$ exists. Choose $0\ne\phi\in C_c^\infty((1,2))$, $\phi\ge0$, and $W(y)=y^{-\varrho}\phi(y)$. With the Mellin convention $\widehat W(s)=\int_0^\infty W(y)y^s\,dy/y$, we have $\widehat W(\varrho)=\int\phi(y)\,dy/y>0$. The function $$\mathcal M_W(s)=\int_0^\infty A_1(D)D^{-s}\frac{\,dD}{D}$$ is holomorphic on $\operatorname{Re}s>11/12$: the estimate <a href="#eq:mobius-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mobius-saving">[eq:mobius-saving]</a> controls the integral at infinity, and the compact support of $W$ makes $A_1(D)$ vanish for sufficiently small $D$. Termwise integration for $\operatorname{Re}s>1$ gives $$\mathcal M_W(s)
 =\widehat W(s)\sum_{(n,S)=1}\frac{\mu(n)\nu(n)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)^s}
 =\frac{\widehat W(s)}{L_K^S(s,\nu)},$$ where $L_K^S$ is the Euler product outside $S$: $$L_K^S(s,\nu):=L_K(s,\nu)
 \prod_{\mathfrak p\in S}(1-\nu(\mathfrak p)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak p)^{-s}).$$ By Hecke’s meromorphic continuation theorem (see <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), 5.10]) and the identity theorem, the identity $L_K^S(s,\nu)\mathcal M_W(s)=\widehat W(s)$ holds on $\operatorname{Re}s>11/12$. The omitted Euler factors are nonzero here, so evaluation at $s=\varrho$ gives $0=\widehat W(\varrho)>0$, a contradiction.

To deduce the assertion for Dirichlet $L$-functions in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, let $\chi_{-3}$ be the nontrivial character modulo $3$. For any Dirichlet character $\chi$, quadratic base change gives, up to Euler factors nonzero in $\operatorname{Re}s>0$, $$L_K(s,\chi\circ\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q})
       =L(s,\chi)L(s,\chi\chi_{-3}).$$ The Hecke conclusion excludes zeros of either factor away from $s=1$. At $s=1$, the only possible pole–zero cancellation is ruled out by Dirichlet’s nonvanishing theorem, which gives $L(1,\chi_{-3})>0$ (see <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[Dav00](#ref-Dav00), Chapters 4 and 6]). ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:initialization"></a>
### Poisson summation and the dual mean square

We now turn to the mean-square estimate in Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a>. Following Step 3 of the outline, we expand the square and apply Poisson summation in the row variable $u$. The finite Fourier transforms of the characters supply Gauss sums. Arithmetic identities then combine these Gauss sums with the original Möbius coefficients to give the normalized cubic Gauss-sum coefficients that appear in the theta function.

The column indices produced by expanding the square need not be coprime. We first extract their common factor, then use Möbius inversion to separate the remaining coprimality condition. These operations introduce an auxiliary twisting index and an exclusion ideal. The comparison below removes the exclusion without changing the row range or the product of the column and auxiliary scales.

We record the arithmetic and Poisson identities first, then define this family. Proposition <a href="#prop:poisson-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:poisson-reduction">8</a> states the estimate for it that suffices to prove <a href="#eq:ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ms">[eq:ms]</a>; the rest of the section proves that implication.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The arithmetic identities

We first record the identities that convert between Möbius coefficients and normalized cubic Gauss sums. Recall that, for a squarefree primary element $n$ with $(n,S)=1$, $$\alpha(n)=\frac{n}{|n|},\qquad
 \gamma_j(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)}}
       \sum_{x\bmod n}\chi_n(x)^j e(x/n)\quad(j\in\mathbb Z),$$ where $e(z)=\exp(4\pi\mathrm i\,\operatorname{Im}z/\sqrt3)$, as in <a href="#eq:intro-gauss-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-gauss-sum">[eq:intro-gauss-sum]</a>. Put $$a_\xi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\xi(n)
 \qquad(n\text{ squarefree}),$$ where $\xi$ is a fixed ray class character. The character $\xi$ accounts for the original twist $\nu$ and for the residue-class factors in the identities below.

By character orthogonality <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), 3.1], we can absorb functions on a fixed ray class group into a finite sum of twists $\xi$. Choose a fixed modulus, supported on $S$, divisible by the conductor of $\nu$ and sufficiently large that all reciprocity factors below depend only on the corresponding ray classes. We then expand these factors in characters of this finite group.

The following lemma collects standard consequences of the Gauss–Jacobi identities, quadratic Gauss-sum evaluations, and reciprocity.

<a id="stmt-lem-arithmetic"></a>
<div id="lem:arithmetic" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:recip"></a>
<a id="eq:gj"></a>
<a id="eq:g"></a>
<a id="eq:convert1"></a>
<a id="eq:convert2"></a>
<a id="eq:crt-a"></a>
<a id="eq:quotient"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *On a fixed ray class group, there are a function $G$ with values in $\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|=1\}$ and a symmetric $\{\pm1\}$-valued bicharacter $\mathcal R$. This means that $\mathcal R(a,b)=\mathcal R(b,a)$ and $\mathcal R$ is multiplicative in each argument separately: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal R(aa',b)&=\mathcal R(a,b)\mathcal R(a',b),\\
 \mathcal R(a,bb')&=\mathcal R(a,b)\mathcal R(a,b'),
 \end{aligned}$$ for all classes $a,a',b,b'$ in the ray class group. These functions have the following properties. Let $a,b,n$ be primary elements prime to $S$, with $(a,b)=1$ and $n$ squarefree. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 \chi_b(a)&=\mathcal R(a,b)\chi_a(b),&
 G(ab)&=G(a)G(b)\mathcal R(a,b),                                      \label{eq:recip}\\
 \gamma_2(n)^3&=\mu(n)\alpha(n),&
 \gamma_1(n)\gamma_2(n)&=\mu(n)\alpha(n)G(n),                 \label{eq:gj}\\
 G(n)&=\overline{\chi_n(4)}\gamma_3(n),&
 \gamma_1(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)&=\chi_n(-1).                     \label{eq:g}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\gamma_1(n)&=\mu(n)G(n),\label{eq:convert1}\\
 \mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)
 &=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n),\label{eq:convert2}\\
 a_\xi(ab)&=a_\xi(a)a_\xi(b)\chi_b(a)^4,                     \label{eq:crt-a}\\
 \chi_a(-1)\overline{G(a)}G(b)\mathcal R(a,b)&=G(ba^{-1}).            \label{eq:quotient}
\end{aligned}$$ In <a href="#eq:crt-a" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crt-a">[eq:crt-a]</a>, $a,b$ are also squarefree. The identity for $G(ab)$ in <a href="#eq:recip" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recip">[eq:recip]</a> extends to all classes of the fixed ray class group; the quotient in <a href="#eq:quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient">[eq:quotient]</a> is taken in that group.*

</div>

The proof, including the dependence of $G$ and $\mathcal R$ on fixed ray classes, is given in Appendix <a href="#app:gauss-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:gauss-identities">8.1</a>. Under Poisson summation, <a href="#eq:convert2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:convert2">[eq:convert2]</a> converts the Möbius coefficients to $a_\xi(n)$, up to fixed ray class factors, while <a href="#eq:convert1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:convert1">[eq:convert1]</a> converts them back.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Poisson summation with excluded primes

The row sum to which we apply Poisson summation will have an additional coprimality restriction. We record the formula with that restriction included, so that its effect on the Fourier frequencies and the normalization is explicit.

For a nonzero ideal $\mathfrak r$, write $\operatorname{rad}\mathfrak r$ for the product of its distinct prime divisors. We use the additive character $e$ introduced in Step 3. In quotients and Gauss sums, use a fixed generator for each ideal, chosen primary when the ideal is prime to $3$.

<a id="stmt-lem-poisson"></a>
<div id="lem:poisson" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:poisson"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *Let $\mathcal H>0$, let $\mathfrak r$ be a nonzero ideal, and let $\Phi(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)/\mathcal H)$ be a smooth radial Schwartz weight on the row lattice. Let $\chi$ be a primitive multiplicative character of $(\mathcal O/\mathfrak m)^\times$, viewed as a function on $\mathcal O$ by reduction modulo $\mathfrak m$ and extension by zero on nonunits. Write $$\gamma(\chi)=\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak m)^{-1/2}
\sum_{x\bmod\mathfrak m}\chi(x)e(x/\mathfrak m)$$ for its normalized Gauss sum. Since $\chi$ is primitive, its modulus $\mathfrak m$ is determined by $\chi$ and is suppressed in the notation. Then $$\label{eq:poisson}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\sum_k\chi(k)\mathbf 1_{(k,\mathfrak r)=1}\Phi(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)/\mathcal{H})\\
 &\quad=\frac{\mathcal{H}\gamma(\chi)}{\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak m)}}
   \sum_{d\mid\operatorname{rad}\mathfrak r}\frac{\mu(d)\chi(d)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)}
   \sum_h\overline{\chi(h)}
        \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal{H}\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak m)}\Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $\widehat\Phi$ is defined by $$\widehat\Phi(|w|^2)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\int_{\mathbb C}
       \Phi(|z|^2)e(-zw)\,dx\,dy,\qquad z=x+\mathrm i y.$$ The measure is normalized so that $\mathcal O$ has covolume one. Here $k,h\in\mathcal O$, and $h$ is the Fourier frequency. We have $$\chi\text{ nonprincipal}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \overline{\chi(0)}=0.$$ For the principal primitive character ($\chi=\mathbf1$, $\mathfrak m=1$), the zero-frequency contribution is $$\mathcal H\widehat\Phi(0)
   \prod_{\mathfrak p\mid \mathfrak r}\Bigl(1-\frac1{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak p)}\Bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-poisson"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-poisson</summary>

*Proof.* Inclusion–exclusion followed by $k=d\ell$ gives $$\sum_k\chi(k)\mathbf 1_{(k,\mathfrak r)=1}\Phi(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)/\mathcal{H})
 =\sum_{d\mid\operatorname{rad}\mathfrak r}\mu(d)\chi(d)
       \sum_\ell\chi(\ell)\Phi\!\Bigl(\frac{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\ell)}{\mathcal{H}/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)}\Bigr).$$ Apply lattice Poisson summation <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), Theorem 4.5] in $\ell$ on residue classes modulo $\mathfrak m$, at scale $\mathcal H/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)$. The primitive Gauss-sum identity <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), (3.12)] evaluates the finite Fourier transform as $\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak m)}\gamma(\chi)\overline{\chi(h)}$ for every $h$, giving <a href="#eq:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:poisson">[eq:poisson]</a>. For a nonprincipal primitive character, $\overline{\chi(0)}=0$, so the zero-frequency term vanishes. For the principal character, sum $\mu(d)/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)$ over $d\mid\operatorname{rad}\mathfrak r$ to obtain the displayed zero-frequency contribution. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The dual mean squares

We use the following mean square of the column sums with coefficients $a_\xi(n)$.

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

<a id="eq:energy"></a>
**Definition 6**. The parameters $\mathcal H$, $X$, and $F$ are the norm scales of $k$, $n$, and $f$, respectively. Here $k$ ranges over elements of $\mathcal O$, and a star restricts a sum to squarefree ideals prime to $S$, represented by their primary generators. For a smooth compactly supported weight $W$ on $(0,\infty)$, define the *dual mean square* by $$\label{eq:energy}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\mathcal E(\mathcal{H},X,F;\xi,W)\\
 &\qquad=\frac1{XF}
 \sum_{\substack{F\le\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(f)<2F\\(f,S)=1}}^*\sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)\le \mathcal{H}}
 \Bigl|\sum_{(n,S)=1}^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
                  W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X)\Bigr|^2.
 \end{aligned}$$

</div>

Below, $\xi$ ranges over all characters of the fixed ray class group chosen above, through which $\nu$, $G$, and $\mathcal R$ factor.

Write $\mathcal E_{\mathfrak r}$ for the same expression with the additional restriction $(n,\mathfrak r)=1$. This notation is only needed in the Poisson reductions; the following comparison returns to $\mathcal E$.

<a id="stmt-lem-remove-exclusions"></a>
<div id="lem:remove-exclusions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *Let $r_0$ be the product of the primes dividing $\mathfrak r$ outside $S$. For $\mathcal H,X>0$, $F\ge1$, and smooth compactly supported $W$, <a id="eq:remove-exclusions"></a>$$\label{eq:remove-exclusions}
 \mathcal E_{\mathfrak r}(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \le\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(r_0)
 \sum_{d\mid r_0}\mathcal E\!\Bigl(\mathcal H,\frac X{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)},F\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d);\xi,W\Bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-remove-exclusions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-remove-exclusions</summary>

*Proof.* Let $C_{\mathfrak r}(X;k,f)$ denote the inner column sum defining $\mathcal E_{\mathfrak r}$, with $\xi,W$ fixed. Inclusion–exclusion, $n=dm$, and <a href="#eq:crt-a" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crt-a">[eq:crt-a]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 C_{\mathfrak r}(X;k,f)
 &=\sum_{d\mid r_0}\mu(d)
   \sum_{\substack{(n,S)=1\\d\mid n}}^*
   a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X)\\
 &=\sum_{\substack{d\mid r_0\\(d,f)=1}}
   \mu(d)a_\xi(d)\chi_d(k)\chi_d(f)^4
   C_1(X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d);k,df).
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $\chi_m(d)^4$ enforces $(m,d)=1$ and combines with $\chi_m(f)^4$; terms with $(d,f)\ne1$ vanish. Each exterior coefficient has modulus at most one. Apply Cauchy–Schwarz in $d$, then enlarge the injective image $f\mapsto df$ to the squarefree range $F\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)\le\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(df)<2F\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d)$. The normalizing product is unchanged: $(X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d))(F\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(d))=XF$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Reduction to the dual mean square

The following proposition gives the dual estimate sufficient for <a href="#eq:ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ms">[eq:ms]</a>. Its proof applies Poisson summation directly to the original Möbius sums.

<a id="stmt-prop-poisson-reduction"></a>
<div id="prop:poisson-reduction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:initial-scales"></a>
<a id="eq:auxiliary-target"></a>
**Proposition 8**. *Fix $0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and put $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$. For each fixed $C\ge1$ and all real $B,F\ge1$, consider the ranges $$\label{eq:initial-scales}
 X=\frac D{BF},\qquad 0<\mathcal{H}\le\frac{CD^2}{HB^2},\qquad
 XF=\frac DB.$$ Suppose that for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists an integer $J=J(\vartheta,\varepsilon)\ge1$ such that, for every compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and every smooth $W$ supported in $I$, $$\label{eq:auxiliary-target}
 \mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{\nu,S,I,C,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2D^\varepsilon XF,$$ where $$\|W\|_{C^J(I)}=\max_{0\le j\le J}\sup_{x\in I}|W^{(j)}(x)|.$$ For functions of several variables, the norm uses all partial derivatives of total order at most $J$. Then the original mean-square estimate in Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a> holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-poisson-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-poisson-reduction</summary>

<a id="eq:expanded-ms"></a>
<a id="eq:initial-poisson-rows"></a>
*Proof.* Write $I=[a_0,b_0]$. Choose a nonnegative radial Schwartz weight $\Phi$ such that $\Phi(x)\ge1$ on $[0,1]$ and $\operatorname{supp}\widehat\Phi\subset[0,C_\Phi]$ for some fixed $C_\Phi>0$. It suffices to prove $\mathcal M_D\ll_{\nu,S,I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
\|W\|_{C^{J'}(I)}^2HD^\varepsilon$ for some $J'=J'(\vartheta,\varepsilon)$, where $$\label{eq:expanded-ms}
 \mathcal M_D:=\frac1D\sum_{u\in\mathcal O}\Phi(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)/H)
 \Bigl|\sum_{(n,S)=1}\mu(n)\nu(n)\chi_n(u)W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/D)\Bigr|^2.$$ Conjugate the inner sum, expand the square, and put $g=(n_1,n_2)$ and $n_j=gz_j$. The squarefree indices satisfy $(z_1,z_2)=(z_1z_2,g)=1$, and $$\overline{\chi_{n_1}(u)}\chi_{n_2}(u)
 =\mathbf 1_{(u,g)=1}\overline{\chi_{z_1}(u)}\chi_{z_2}(u).$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:poisson">5</a> with $\chi=\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}$ and exclusion $g$: $$\label{eq:initial-poisson-rows}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\sum_u\Phi(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)/H)\mathbf 1_{(u,g)=1}\chi(u)\\
 &\quad=\sum_{e\mid g}
 \frac{H\mu(e)\chi(e)\gamma(\chi)}
      {\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(e)\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(z_1)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(z_2)}}
 \sum_h\overline{\chi(h)}
 \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(\frac{H\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(h)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(e)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(z_1)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(z_2)}\Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ The zero frequency occurs only when $z_1=z_2=1$ and contributes $$Z=\frac HD\widehat\Phi(0)
 \sum_{(g,S)=1}^*|W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(g)/D)|^2
       \prod_{p\mid g}\Bigl(1-\frac1{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)}\Bigr)
 \ll_{\Phi,I}H\|W\|_\infty^2.$$ Retain all $h\ne0$, including those with $z_1=z_2=1$.

<a id="eq:initial-paired-gauss"></a>
Expand the fixed ray class function $\overline{\nu(t)}G(t^{-1})=\sum_\xi c_\xi\xi(t)$. The Chinese remainder theorem and <a href="#eq:convert2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:convert2">[eq:convert2]</a>–<a href="#eq:quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient">[eq:quotient]</a> give $$\label{eq:initial-paired-gauss}
 \mu(z_1)\mu(z_2)\overline{\nu(z_1)}\nu(z_2)
       \gamma(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}) =\sum_\xi c_\xi a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}.$$ Write $N=\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}$ for the remainder of this proof, and put $W_0(x)=x^{-1/2}\overline{W(x)}$. Substituting <a href="#eq:initial-paired-gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-paired-gauss">[eq:initial-paired-gauss]</a> into <a href="#eq:initial-poisson-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-poisson-rows">[eq:initial-poisson-rows]</a>, including the normalization $1/D$ in <a href="#eq:expanded-ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:expanded-ms">[eq:expanded-ms]</a>, gives $$\mathcal M_D-Z=\sum_\xi c_\xi\mathcal S_\xi,
 \qquad
 |\mathcal M_D-Z|\ll\max_\xi|\mathcal S_\xi|,$$ since the character sum is fixed and finite. Fix $\xi$. Using $\overline{\chi_z(e)}=\chi_z(e^5)$, its contribution is $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal S_\xi
 ={}&\frac H{D^2}
 \sum_g^*\sum_{e\mid g}\frac{\mu(e)N(g)}{N(e)}
 \sum_{h\ne0}
 \sum_{\substack{z_1,z_2\\(z_1,z_2)=1\\(z_1z_2,g)=1}}^*
 a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}
 \chi_{z_1}(he^5)\overline{\chi_{z_2}(he^5)}
 \\
 &\quad{}\times
 W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(gz_1)}D\Bigr)
 \overline{W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(gz_2)}D\Bigr)}
 \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(
 \frac{HN(h)}{N(e)N(z_1)N(z_2)}
 \Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here and below every starred variable is squarefree, primary, and prime to $S$, while $h,k$ range over nonzero elements of $\mathcal O$. We must show $|\mathcal S_\xi|\ll_{\nu,S,I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
\|W\|_{C^{J'}(I)}^2HD^\varepsilon$.

<a id="eq:initial-column-output"></a>
Insert the coprimality identity and change variables: $$\mathbf 1_{(z_1,z_2)=1}
 =\sum_{v\mid z_1,\ v\mid z_2}\mu(v),
 \qquad z_j=vm_j.$$ By <a href="#eq:crt-a" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crt-a">[eq:crt-a]</a>, the common factor from the two columns satisfies $$a_\xi(vm)=a_\xi(v)a_\xi(m)\chi_m(v)^4,
 \qquad
 |a_\xi(v)\chi_v(he^5)|^2=\mathbf 1_{(v,h)=1},$$ where $(v,e)=1$. Thus the same sum becomes $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal S_\xi
 ={}&\frac H{D^2}
 \sum_{\substack{g,v\\(g,v)=1}}^*
 \sum_{e\mid g}\frac{\mu(e)\mu(v)N(g)}{N(e)}
 \sum_{\substack{h\ne0\\(h,v)=1}}
 \sum_{\substack{m_1,m_2\\(m_1m_2,gv)=1}}^*
 a_\xi(m_1)\overline{a_\xi(m_2)}
 \\
 &\quad{}\times
 \chi_{m_1}(he^5v^4)\overline{\chi_{m_2}(he^5v^4)}
 W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(gvm_1)}D\Bigr)
 \overline{W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(gvm_2)}D\Bigr)}
 \\
 &\quad{}\times
 \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(
 \frac{HN(h)}{N(e)N(v)^2N(m_1)N(m_2)}
 \Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ There is now no restriction $(m_1,m_2)=1$. Make the bijective change of variables $$\begin{gathered}
 b=g/e,\qquad f=ev,\qquad k=eh,\\
 e=(f,k),\qquad v=f/e,\qquad g=be,\qquad h=k/e.
 \end{gathered}$$ The resulting $b,f$ are coprime and squarefree, $k\ne0$ is arbitrary, and $$gv=bf,\qquad he^5v^4=kf^4,\qquad
 \mu(e)\mu(v)=\mu(f),\qquad
 \frac{N(g)}{N(e)}=N(b).$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:initial-column-output}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal S_\xi
 ={}&\frac H{D^2}
 \sum_{\substack{b,f\\(b,f)=1}}^*\mu(f)N(b)
 \sum_{k\ne0}
 \sum_{\substack{m_1,m_2\\(m_1m_2,b)=1}}^*
 a_\xi(m_1)\overline{a_\xi(m_2)}
 \chi_{m_1}(kf^4)\overline{\chi_{m_2}(kf^4)}
 \\
 &\quad{}\times
 W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(bfm_1)}D\Bigr)
 \overline{W_0\!\Bigl(\frac{N(bfm_2)}D\Bigr)}
 \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(
 \frac{HN(k)}{N(f)^2N(m_1)N(m_2)}
 \Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ The characters enforce $(m_j,f)=1$, leaving only the displayed exclusion $(m_j,b)=1$. The supports of $W_0$ and $\widehat\Phi$ give $$N(b)N(f)\le b_0D,\qquad
 0<N(k)\le\frac{C_\Phi b_0^2D^2}{HN(b)^2}.$$

Partition $B\le N(b)<2B$ and $F\le N(f)<2F$ into dyadic ranges. Denote the corresponding contribution to <a href="#eq:initial-column-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-column-output">[eq:initial-column-output]</a> by $\mathcal S_{\xi;B,F}$, and put $$X=\frac D{BF},\qquad
 \mathcal H=\frac{C_{I,\Phi}D^2}{HB^2},\qquad
 C_{I,\Phi}=\max(2,C_\Phi b_0^2).$$ These satisfy <a href="#eq:initial-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-scales">[eq:initial-scales]</a>. In the variables $x_j=N(m_j)/X$, the coupled smooth weight is $$\begin{gathered}
 \mathcal K_{b,f,k}(x_1,x_2)
 =W_0(r x_1)\overline{W_0(r x_2)}
 \widehat\Phi\!\Bigl(\frac{a}{x_1x_2}\Bigr),\\
 r=\frac{N(b)N(f)}{BF}\in[1,4),\qquad
 a=\frac{HN(k)}{N(f)^2X^2}\le C_{I,\Phi}.
 \end{gathered}$$ For every integer $q\ge0$, these kernels satisfy $$\sup_{b,f,k}\|\mathcal K_{b,f,k}\|_{C^q([a_0/4,b_0]^2)}
 \ll_{I,\Phi,q}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2.$$ For $U\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$, where $I_*=[a_0/8,2b_0]$, put $$S_U(b,f,k)=\sum_{\substack{(n,S)=1\\(n,b)=1}}^*a_\xi(n)
       \chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4U(N(n)/X).$$ The shifts $(X,F)\mapsto(X/N(d),FN(d))$ preserve <a href="#eq:initial-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-scales">[eq:initial-scales]</a>. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:remove-exclusions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:remove-exclusions">7</a> and <a href="#eq:auxiliary-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary-target">[eq:auxiliary-target]</a>, with $J=J(\vartheta,\varepsilon/4)$, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{f,k}|S_U(b,f,k)|^2
 &=XF\,\mathcal E_{(b)}(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,U)\\
 &\le XF\,\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)
       \sum_{d\mid b}\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X/N(d),FN(d);\xi,U)\\
 &\ll_{\nu,S,I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
 D^{\varepsilon/2}(XF)^2\|U\|_{C^J(I_*)}^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $N(b)\ll_I D$, so the divisor factors are absorbed in $D^{\varepsilon/4}$. We use the following smooth-weight principle, stated and proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:smooth-mean-square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth-mean-square">23</a>: a mean-square bound valid for every common test function, with a $C^J$ norm, also bounds the corresponding quadratic sum with a kernel depending on the row, at a cost given by the kernel’s $C^{2J+4}$ norm. For each fixed $b$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:smooth-mean-square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth-mean-square">23</a> with row index $(f,k)$, coefficient $\mu(f)\mathbf 1_{(f,b)=1}$, and kernel $\mathcal K_{b,f,k}$. The larger row range $0<N(k)\le\mathcal H$ adds only terms whose original kernel vanishes. With $q=2J+4$, this gives <a id="eq:weighted"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal S_{\xi;B,F}|
 &\ll_{\nu,S,I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
 D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2
 \sum_{B\le N(b)<2B}^*\frac{HN(b)}{D^2}(XF)^2\\
 &\ll D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2
 \frac{HB}{D^2}\,B\,(XF)^2
 =D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2H,
 \end{aligned}$$ where $XF=D/B$ and ideal counting gives $O(B)$ choices of $b$. Summing the $O((\log D)^2)$ nonempty dyadic ranges bounds $\mathcal S_\xi$ as required. Sum over the fixed finite set of $\xi$, include $Z$, and restore the factor $D$ to obtain <a href="#eq:ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ms">[eq:ms]</a>. The required derivative order depends only on $\vartheta,\varepsilon$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:descent"></a>
### Iteration of the dual mean-square estimate

Put $\Sigma=XF$. We prove the following estimate for the family <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a>, with exclusions removed by Lemma <a href="#lem:remove-exclusions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:remove-exclusions">7</a>. The two inputs are proved in Sections <a href="#sec:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reflection">6</a> and <a href="#sec:transfer-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer-proof">7</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-canonical"></a>
<div id="prop:canonical" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9**. *Fix $\kappa>0$ and $C_0\ge1$. Suppose that <a id="eq:positive-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:positive-gap}
 \mathcal H,X,F\ge1,\qquad
 \Sigma=XF\le D^{C_0},\qquad
 \mathcal H\le\Sigma D^{-\kappa}.$$ For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is an integer $J=J(\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon)\ge1$ such that <a id="eq:canonical-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:canonical-bound}
 \mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon}
 \|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2D^\varepsilon\Sigma$$ for every compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and smooth $W$ supported in $I$.*

</div>

The proof is given in Section <a href="#sec:canonical-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:canonical-proof">5.5</a>, using Lemma <a href="#lem:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cube-reduction">11</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">12</a>.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The completed sums

<a id="eq:T"></a>
Let $\Psi$ be a completely multiplicative $\mathbb C$-valued function on the nonzero integral ideals of $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[\omega]$ coprime to $3$, vanishing on ideals divisible by a prime in $S$. For a primary element $n$, write $\Psi(n)=\Psi((n))$. Define $$\label{eq:T}
 T(X;\Psi)=
 \sum_{(n,S)=1}^*\sum_{\substack{b\in\mathcal O\\b\equiv1\ (3)\\(b,S)=1}}
 \frac{\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)
       \overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\Psi(b)^3}
      {\sqrt{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)}\,\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)}\,V_*(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3/X),$$ where $V_*(y)=\sqrt y\,W(y)$. The extra index $b$ supplies the cubes in the Fourier expansion of the Kubota theta function. Here $n,b$ are primary elements of $\mathcal O$; only $n$ is required to be squarefree.

<a id="eq:completed-twist"></a>
The $b=1$ part is exactly $X^{-1/2}\sum_{(n,S)=1}^*\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)W(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)/X)$. To identify this with the normalized column sum in <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a>, fix a ray class character $\xi$. For a nonzero row $k\in\mathcal O$ and a squarefree primary $f$ prime to $S$, set $$\label{eq:completed-twist}
 \Psi_k(n):=\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4,
 \qquad T(X;k,f):=T(X;\Psi_k).$$ The displayed product defines $\Psi_k(n)$ for $(n,S)=1$; set $\Psi_k(n)=0$ otherwise. Thus the zero extension required in <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a> is part of this definition.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The completed mean-square estimate

For the twist <a href="#eq:completed-twist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completed-twist">[eq:completed-twist]</a>, the theta transformation converts the relevant sextic twists into quadratic characters. Combining it with the quadratic large sieve gives the following estimate, proved in Section <a href="#sec:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reflection">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-r"></a>
<div id="prop:R" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:R"></a>
**Proposition 10**. *Fix $\varepsilon>0$, $C_0\ge1$, and a character $\xi$ of the fixed ray class group. There is $J=J(\varepsilon,C_0)\ge1$ such that $$\label{eq:R}
 \sum_{0<\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(k)\ll\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2
 \ll_{I,\xi,S,\varepsilon,C_0}
 D^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2
 \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(f)}{X}\Bigr)$$ whenever $$1\le\mathcal H,X,\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(f)\le D^{C_0}.$$ Here $f$ is squarefree and primary with $(f,S)=1$, $W$ is smooth and supported in a compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$, and $T$ is defined by <a href="#eq:completed-twist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completed-twist">[eq:completed-twist]</a>.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Cube inversion and the remaining sums

Set <a id="eq:cube-cutoff"></a>$$\label{eq:cube-cutoff}
 H_c^3=\min\!\Bigl(X,\frac{X^2}{\mathcal H^2}\Bigr),
 \qquad L_b=\frac{X}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-cube-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:cube-reduction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cube-reduction"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *Fix $C_0\ge1$ and $\varepsilon>0$. Suppose $1\le\mathcal H,X,F\le D^{C_0}$ and $\mathcal H\le\Sigma=XF$. There is an integer $J=J(\varepsilon,C_0)\ge1$ such that $$\label{eq:cube-reduction}
 \mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,C_0,\varepsilon}D^\varepsilon
 \biggl(\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2+
 \sup_{\substack{b\equiv1\ (3),\ (b,S)=1\\
                  \mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)>H_c,\ L_b>1}}
       \mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)\biggr)$$ for every compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$. An empty supremum is zero; in particular it is empty when $\mathcal H^2\le X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cube-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cube-reduction</summary>

<a id="eq:cube-inverse"></a>
*Proof.* Write $N=\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}$ and $I=[u,v]$. Complete multiplicativity in <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a>, including at the zeros of $\Psi_k$, gives $$\label{eq:cube-inverse}
 \begin{aligned}
 &X^{-1/2}\sum_{(n,S)=1}^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
 W(N(n)/X)\\
 &\qquad=\sum_{(h,S)=1}
 \frac{\mu(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,3}\Psi_k(h)^3}{N(h)}
 T(X/N(h)^3;k,f).
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, after substitution of <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a>, the coefficient of the total cube index $b$ contains $\sum_{h\mid b}\mu(h)=\mathbf 1_{b=1}$. This is Möbius inversion; compare <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), (1.18)] and <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[DR](#ref-DR), (8.2)].

Split the right-hand side of <a href="#eq:cube-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-inverse">[eq:cube-inverse]</a> into $P_{\rm short}(k,f)+P_{\rm long}(k,f)$, where $$\begin{aligned}
 P_{\rm short}(k,f)&=
 \sum_{\substack{(h,S)=1\\N(h)\le H_c}}
 \frac{\mu(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,3}\Psi_k(h)^3}{N(h)}
 T(X/N(h)^3;k,f),\\
 \mathcal E_{\rm short}&=\frac1F\sum_{\substack{F\le N(f)<2F\\(f,S)=1}}^*
 \sum_{0<N(k)\le\mathcal H}|P_{\rm short}(k,f)|^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Define $\mathcal E_{\rm long}$ in the same way using $P_{\rm long}$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz for each $k,f$, followed by Proposition <a href="#prop:R" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:R">10</a>, gives <a id="eq:short-cube-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:short-cube-bound}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E_{\rm short}
 &\le\Bigl(\sum_{N(h)\le H_c}\frac1{N(h)}\Bigr)
 \sum_{N(h)\le H_c}\frac1{N(h)} \frac1F\sum_{\substack{F\le N(f)<2F\\(f,S)=1}}^*
 \sum_{0<N(k)\le\mathcal H}|T(X/N(h)^3;k,f)|^2\\
 &\ll_{I,\nu,S,C_0,\varepsilon}
 D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2
 \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2FH_c^3}{X}\Bigr)
 \ll D^{\varepsilon/2}\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $X/N(h)^3\ge1$, and the reciprocal-norm sum contributes only a logarithm. We use Proposition <a href="#prop:R" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:R">10</a> with exponent $\varepsilon/4$ and range $C_0+1$, since $N(f)<2D^{C_0}$. If $H_c<1$, the sum is empty.

Expand $T$ using <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a> in the remaining terms, with cube index $c$: $$\begin{aligned}
 P_{\rm long}(k,f)
 ={}&\frac1{\sqrt X}
 \sum_{\substack{(h,S)=1\\N(h)>H_c}}\sum_{(c,S)=1}
 \mu(h)\sqrt{N(hc)}\,\overline{\alpha(hc)}^{\,3}\Psi_k(hc)^3\\
 &\quad\times\sum_{(n,S)=1}^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
 W\!\Bigl(\frac{N(n)N(hc)^3}{X}\Bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ Put $b=hc$; the pairs giving $b$ are exactly $(h,b/h)$ with $h\mid b$ and $N(h)>H_c$. Since $\Psi_k(b)^3=\xi(b)^3\chi_b(k)^3\mathbf 1_{(b,f)=1}$, this gives $$\begin{aligned}
 P_{\rm long}(k,f)
 ={}&\sum_{\substack{(b,S)=1\\N(b)>H_c}}
 \frac{\beta_0(b,f)\chi_b(k)^3}{N(b)}
 \frac1{\sqrt{L_b}}
 \sum_{(n,S)=1}^*a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
 W(N(n)/L_b),\\
 \beta_0(b,f):={}&\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\xi(b)^3
 \mathbf 1_{(b,f)=1}\sum_{\substack{h\mid b\\N(h)>H_c}}\mu(h),
 \qquad |\beta_0(b,f)|\le\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b).
 \end{aligned}$$ The divisor function $\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)$ counts ideal divisors. All these indices are primary and prime to $S$; $b$ need not be squarefree. The support of $W$ restricts the sum to $N(b)^3\le vX$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E_{\rm long}
 &\le\Bigl(\sum_{\substack{H_c<N(b)\le(vX)^{1/3}\\(b,S)=1}}
             \frac{\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)}{N(b)}\Bigr)
       \sum_{\substack{H_c<N(b)\le(vX)^{1/3}\\(b,S)=1}}
             \frac{\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)}{N(b)}
             \mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W).
 \end{aligned}$$ The reciprocal divisor sum is $\ll_I\log^2(2+X)$, since $$\sum_{N(b)\le R}\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{div}}(b)}{N(b)}
 =\sum_{N(cd)\le R}\frac1{N(c)N(d)}
 \le\Bigl(\sum_{N(c)\le R}\frac1{N(c)}\Bigr)^2
 \ll\log^2(2R)\qquad(R\ge1).$$ If $L_b\le1$, the column sum is empty unless $L_b\ge1/v$; otherwise it has $O_I(1)$ terms, so $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_I\frac{\mathcal H}{L_b}\|W\|_\infty^2
 \ll_I\Sigma\|W\|_\infty^2.$$ Absorb the logarithms in $D^\varepsilon$ and combine the two parts using $\mathcal E\le2\mathcal E_{\rm short}+2\mathcal E_{\rm long}$. This proves <a href="#eq:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-reduction">[eq:cube-reduction]</a>. If $\mathcal H^2\le X$, then $H_c=X^{1/3}$, so $N(b)>H_c$ implies $L_b<1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The transfer estimate

<a id="eq:smoothed-energy"></a>
Fix a nonnegative radial Schwartz weight $\Phi$ with $\Phi(t)\ge1$ for $0\le t\le1$ and $\operatorname{supp}\widehat\Phi\subset[0,C_\Phi]$. Such a weight is obtained by squaring and rescaling a real radial Schwartz function with compactly supported Fourier transform. Write $N=\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}$. For fixed $\mathcal H,L,F,\xi$, put $$\label{eq:smoothed-energy}
 \mathcal A(W):=\frac1{LF}
 \sum_{\substack{F\le N(f)<2F\\(f,S)=1}}^*
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal O}\Phi(N(k)/\mathcal H)
 \Bigl|\sum_{(n,S)=1}^*
 a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(N(n)/L)\Bigr|^2.$$ Thus $\mathcal E(\mathcal H,L,F;\xi,W)\le\mathcal A(W)$. In the following proposition $\Sigma$ is an independent target scale; we will apply it at column scale $L_b$ with $\Sigma=XF$.

<a id="stmt-prop-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:transfer-summary"></a>
<a id="eq:transfer-scales"></a>
**Proposition 12**. *Assume $1\le\mathcal H,L,F,\Sigma\le D^{C_0}$ and $\max\{\mathcal H,LF\}\le\Sigma$, for fixed $C_0\ge1$. For every integer $m\ge0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, $$\label{eq:transfer-summary}
 \mathcal A(W)\ll D^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{4m+12}(I)}^2
 \Bigl(1+\sup\frac{\mathcal E(\mathcal H',X',F';\xi',U)}{\Sigma'}\Bigr)$$ where the supremum is over the family <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a>, with $\Sigma'=X'F'$, $\mathcal H',X',F'\ge1$, and $$\label{eq:transfer-scales}
 \mathcal H'\le\frac{\mathcal HL}{\Sigma F},\qquad
 \frac{\mathcal H'}{\Sigma'}\le
 \frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma},\qquad
 \Sigma'\le L.$$ For $I=[u,v]$, the tests satisfy $U\in C_c^\infty(I')$, $I'=[u/16,4v]$, and $\|U\|_{C^m(I')}\le1$; an empty supremum is zero. The estimate holds for every compact $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$, with implied constant depending on $m,C_0,\varepsilon,I,\nu,S$ and the fixed ray class group.*

</div>

The proof is given in Section <a href="#sec:transfer-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer-proof">7</a>, by combining Lemmas <a href="#lem:first-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:first-transfer">19</a> and <a href="#lem:second-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:second-transfer">21</a>.

<a id="sec:canonical-proof"></a>
#### Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:canonical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:canonical">9</a>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:canonical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:canonical">9</a>.* Fix $\kappa>0$ and $C_0\ge1$. We prove by induction on $j\ge0$ that the proposition holds under the additional restriction $\mathcal H\le D^{j\kappa}$, with the derivative order and implied constant allowed to depend on $j$. More precisely, for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is an integer $J=J(j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon)\ge1$ such that $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon}
 D^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2$$ for every compact interval $I=[u,v]\subset(0,\infty)$ and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$, under the hypotheses of the proposition. The derivative order is independent of $I$; this allows us to apply the induction hypothesis on the enlarged interval in the transfer estimate. For the base case $j=0$, we have $\mathcal H\le1$. In <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a>, the support of $W$ restricts $n$ to the $O_I(X)$ ideals with $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)\in[uX,vX]$. The factors $a_\xi(n)$, $\chi_n(k)$, and $\chi_n(f)^4$ have absolute value at most one, so the triangle inequality bounds each inner sum by $O_I(X\|W\|_\infty)$. There are $O(F)$ choices of $f$ and $O(\mathcal H)$ choices of $k$. Consequently, $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_I\frac{F\mathcal H}{XF}
       \bigl(X\|W\|_\infty\bigr)^2
 =\mathcal H X\|W\|_\infty^2.$$ Dividing by $\Sigma=XF$ and using $\mathcal H\le1$ and $F\ge1$ proves the assertion for $j=0$, with $J=1$.

Suppose the assertion holds for $j$, and fix $\varepsilon>0$. Let $m$ be the derivative order supplied by the induction hypothesis with exponent $\varepsilon/3$. For $\mathcal H\le D^{(j+1)\kappa}$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cube-reduction">11</a> with exponent $\varepsilon/3$. We must bound the mean squares in its supremum uniformly in $b$. If this supremum is nonempty, then $\mathcal H^2>X$ and $H_c^3=X^2/\mathcal H^2$. For each such $b$, recall that $L_b=X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3>1$. Since $L_b\le X$ and $\Sigma\ge\max\{\mathcal H,L_bF\}$, Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">12</a> applies at column scale $L_b$, with derivative order $m$, and exponent $\varepsilon/3$. Its estimate also bounds $\mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)$ because $\mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)\le\mathcal A(W)$.

Let $\mathcal H',X',F'$ be any parameters in the supremum in <a href="#eq:transfer-summary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-summary">[eq:transfer-summary]</a>, with $\Sigma'=X'F'$ as in that proposition. By <a href="#eq:transfer-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-scales">[eq:transfer-scales]</a> and $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)>H_c$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal H'
 &\le\frac{\mathcal H L_b}{\Sigma F}
 =\frac{\mathcal H}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^3F^2}
 <\mathcal H\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma}\Bigr)^2
 \le D^{-2\kappa}\mathcal H\le D^{j\kappa},\\
 \frac{\mathcal H'}{\Sigma'}
 &\le\frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma}\le D^{-\kappa},
 \qquad \Sigma'\le L_b\le\Sigma\le D^{C_0}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus each of these mean squares is covered by the induction hypothesis. Its weight $U$ is supported in $[u/16,4v]$ and has $C^m$ norm at most one. Applying the induction hypothesis on this interval gives $$\frac{\mathcal E(\mathcal H',X',F';\xi',U)}{\Sigma'}
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon}D^{\varepsilon/3}.$$ The enlarged interval is determined by $I$, so the implied constant has only the permitted dependence on the support. Substituting into <a href="#eq:transfer-summary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-summary">[eq:transfer-summary]</a> yields, uniformly in $b$, $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon}
 D^{2\varepsilon/3}\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{4m+12}(I)}^2.$$ Substitution into <a href="#eq:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-reduction">[eq:cube-reduction]</a> contributes the remaining factor $D^{\varepsilon/3}$. Choose $J$ at least $4m+12$ and at least the derivative order required by Lemma <a href="#lem:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cube-reduction">11</a>. We obtain $$\mathcal E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_{I,\nu,S,j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon}
 D^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2.$$ The choice of $J$ depends only on $j,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon$. If the supremum in <a href="#eq:cube-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cube-reduction">[eq:cube-reduction]</a> is empty, the same bound follows directly from that lemma. This completes the induction.

Finally, take $j=\lceil C_0/\kappa\rceil$. The hypothesis $\mathcal H\le\Sigma\le D^{C_0}$ ensures $\mathcal H\le D^{j\kappa}$, so the induction gives the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
#### The original mean square and the exponent $11/12$

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-transfer-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#thm:ms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:ms">3</a>.* Fix $0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and put $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$. The ranges in <a href="#eq:initial-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-scales">[eq:initial-scales]</a> give <a id="eq:initial-positive-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:initial-positive-gap}
 \mathcal H\le\frac{CD^{1-\vartheta}}{B^2},\qquad
 \Sigma=XF=\frac DB,\qquad
 \frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma}\ll D^{-\vartheta}.$$ For large $D$, Proposition <a href="#prop:canonical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:canonical">9</a> applies with $\kappa=\vartheta/2$ and $C_0=2$. If $X<1$, nonempty support forces $X\gg_I1$, and counting gives $\mathcal E\ll_I\mathcal H\|W\|_\infty^2
\ll\Sigma\|W\|_\infty^2$; if $\mathcal H<1$, the sum is empty. Thus <a href="#eq:auxiliary-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary-target">[eq:auxiliary-target]</a> holds, with derivative order depending only on $\vartheta,\varepsilon$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:poisson-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:poisson-reduction">8</a> proves <a href="#eq:ms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ms">[eq:ms]</a>. Bounded $D$ is again covered by counting. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:reflection"></a>
### Proof of the completed mean-square estimate

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:R" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:R">10</a>. We first express the completed sum <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a> using cubic theta coefficients and state the transformation formula.
