An inactive prime has $j=0$ and contributes only the scalar $1-\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)^{-1}$. Consequently, for each fixed $h_0$ and active set $\mathcal A$, combining the Fourier coefficients $d_\sigma(\ell)$ of $\overline{\theta(H(z,v))}$ with the sums over $h_p\ne0$ gives $$d_\sigma(\ell)\psi(\lambda^4\ell)
 \prod_{p\in\mathcal A}B_{p,j_p}(\lambda^4\ell),$$ up to a scalar independent of $\ell$. These are the arithmetic factors in the dual sum <a href="#eq:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reflection">[eq:reflection]</a>. In particular, $B_{p,1}(\lambda^4\ell)=\chi_p(\lambda^4\ell)^3$ is the quadratic factor used in <a href="#eq:residual-quadratic-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-quadratic-factor">[eq:residual-quadratic-factor]</a>.

<a id="eq:theta-mellin-inversion"></a>
*The archimedean transform.* We now derive the transformed weight $V_*^\sharp$ in <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a> and the scalar multiplying the dual sum. For $\operatorname{Re}s>1$, introduce the Dirichlet series associated with the completed sum <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a>: $$\mathcal T(s,\Psi)=
 \Bigl(\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal O\\n\equiv1\ (3)\\(n,S)=1}}^*
   \overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)^{-s}\Bigr) \Bigl(\sum_{\substack{b\in\mathcal O\\b\equiv1\ (3)\\(b,S)=1}}\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}
   \Psi(b)^3\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^{-3s+1/2}\Bigr).$$ With $V_*$ from <a href="#eq:T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T">[eq:T]</a> and its Mellin transform defined before <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a>, Mellin inversion gives $$\label{eq:theta-mellin-inversion}
 T(X;\Psi)=\frac1{2\pi \mathrm i}\int_{(\sigma)}
 \widehat V_*(s-\tfrac12)\mathcal T(s,\Psi)X^{s-1/2}\,ds,
 \qquad \sigma>1.$$ The identity <a href="#eq:T-theta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:T-theta">[eq:T-theta]</a>, with $\Psi$ in place of $\Psi_k$, identifies these coefficients with those of $\Theta_\Psi$ after inserting the factor $\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}$. We now compute the normalization relating $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$ to the Mellin transform of the derivative of $\Theta_\Psi$.

<a id="eq:bessel-mellin"></a>
For $z=x+\mathrm i y$, use $\partial_{\bar z}=(\partial_x+\mathrm i\partial_y)/2$ and $\partial_z=(\partial_x-\mathrm i\partial_y)/2$. Differentiation in $\bar z$ supplies a factor $\bar\ell$ in each Fourier mode. Combined with the factor $|\ell|^{-1}$ from the Bessel integral below, this produces the required angular factor $\overline{\alpha(\ell)}$. We therefore define, initially for $\operatorname{Re}s>1$, the Mellin transform $$\mathcal J(s)=\int_0^\infty
 \partial_{\bar z}\Theta_\Psi(z,v)\Bigr|_{z=0}
 v^{2s-1}\,dv.$$ For each nonzero Fourier mode, differentiation and the Mellin integral for $K_{1/3}$ (see <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[DLMF](#ref-DLMF), (10.43.19)]) give the following formulas: <a id="eq:fourier-derivative"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \partial_{\bar z}\breve e(\ell z)
   &=2\pi \mathrm i\bar\ell\,\breve e(\ell z),\label{eq:fourier-derivative}\\
 \int_0^\infty v^{2s}K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\,dv
   &=\frac{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s+1/3)\Gamma(s+2/3)}
          {(4\pi|\ell|)^{2s+1}} \label{eq:bessel-mellin} \qquad \operatorname{Re}s>0.
\end{aligned}$$

For $\operatorname{Re}s>1$, the coefficient formula <a href="#eq:intro-theta-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-coefficients">[eq:intro-theta-coefficients]</a> and <a href="#eq:bessel-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bessel-mellin">[eq:bessel-mellin]</a> show that the sum of the integrals of the absolute values is finite. We may therefore integrate the differentiated Fourier series term by term.

<a id="eq:ray-mellin"></a>
For $\ell=\lambda^{-3}nb^3$, the phase and scale simplify to $$\mathrm i\overline{\alpha(\ell)}|\ell|^{-2s}
  =27^s\overline{\alpha(n)}\,
   \overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(n)^{-s}\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(b)^{-3s}.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:intro-theta-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-theta-coefficients">[eq:intro-theta-coefficients]</a> gives $$\label{eq:ray-mellin}
\mathcal J(s)=\frac{3^{5/2}}4
       \Bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^s
       \Gamma(s+1/3)\Gamma(s+2/3)\mathcal T(s,\Psi).$$ We next show that $\mathcal J(s)$ is entire and use <a href="#eq:ray-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-mellin">[eq:ray-mellin]</a> to continue $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$.

Write $(z',v')=g^{-1}(z+a/c,v)$. Differentiating <a href="#eq:theta-cusp-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-cusp-coordinates">[eq:theta-cusp-coordinates]</a> at $z=0$ gives $$\frac{\partial z'}{\partial\bar z}\bigg|_{z=0}
   =-\frac1{c^2v^2},
 \qquad
 \frac{\partial\overline{z'}}{\partial\bar z}\bigg|_{z=0}=0,
 \qquad
 \frac{\partial v'}{\partial\bar z}\bigg|_{z=0}=0.$$ Thus the derivative in cusp coordinates is $-(cv)^{-2}\partial_{z'}$, with no height-derivative term. The defining Fourier series <a href="#eq:general-twisted-theta-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-twisted-theta-definition">[eq:general-twisted-theta-definition]</a> for $\Theta_\Psi$ controls $v\to\infty$. For $v\to0$, use <a href="#eq:theta-cusp-automorphy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-cusp-automorphy">[eq:theta-cusp-automorphy]</a> and the cusp expansions <a href="#eq:cusp-coefficient-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cusp-coefficient-definition">[eq:cusp-coefficient-definition]</a>. In each expansion the horizontal derivative removes the constant mode. The remaining modes decay exponentially as $v\to\infty$; at $v\to0$, the transformed height $1/(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(c)v)$ tends to infinity, giving exponential decay in $1/v$. Thus the integral defining $\mathcal J(s)$ converges for every $s$ and defines an entire function. Equation <a href="#eq:ray-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-mellin">[eq:ray-mellin]</a>, after division by its gamma factors, also continues $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$ to an entire function.

<a id="eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation"></a>
<a id="eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series"></a>
For the term indexed by $\boldsymbol h$ in <a href="#eq:theta-factorized-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-factorized-shifts">[eq:theta-factorized-shifts]</a>, write $c_{\boldsymbol h},\delta'_{\boldsymbol h},g_{1,\boldsymbol h},H_{\boldsymbol h}$ for the corresponding choices above. Let $\sigma_{\boldsymbol h}$ be the cusp index determined by $H_{\boldsymbol h}$. Define its cusp Mellin transform by <a id="eq:dual-cusp-mellin"></a>$$\label{eq:dual-cusp-mellin}
 \mathcal J_{\boldsymbol h}^\vee(s)=\int_0^\infty
 \partial_z\bigl\{\overline{\theta(H_{\boldsymbol h}(z,v))}\bigr\}
 \Bigr|_{z=-\delta'_{\boldsymbol h}/c_{\boldsymbol h}}v^{2s-1}\,dv.$$ Substituting $v\mapsto(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(c_{\boldsymbol h})v)^{-1}$ in each translated term of $\mathcal J(s)$, using <a href="#eq:theta-cusp-automorphy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-cusp-automorphy">[eq:theta-cusp-automorphy]</a>, gives $$\label{eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation}
 \mathcal J(s)=-\sum_{\boldsymbol h} c_{\mathrm F}(\boldsymbol h)\overline{\kappa(g_{1,\boldsymbol h})}\,
       \overline{\alpha(c_{\boldsymbol h})}^{\,2}\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(c_{\boldsymbol h})^{1-2s}\mathcal J_{\boldsymbol h}^\vee(1-s).$$ Here $c_{\mathrm F}(\boldsymbol h)$ is the coefficient in <a href="#eq:theta-factorized-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-factorized-shifts">[eq:theta-factorized-shifts]</a>. The cusp coefficients $d_{\sigma_{\boldsymbol h}}(\ell)$ and <a href="#eq:bessel-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bessel-mellin">[eq:bessel-mellin]</a> give, for $\operatorname{Re}s>1$, $$\label{eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series}
 \mathcal J_{\boldsymbol h}^\vee(s)=\frac{\mathrm i\,\Gamma(s+1/3)\Gamma(s+2/3)}{4(2\pi)^{2s}}
 \sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\mathcal O}
 \frac{d_{\sigma_{\boldsymbol h}}(\ell)\alpha(\ell)}{\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\ell)^s}
 \breve e(-\delta'_{\boldsymbol h}\ell/c_{\boldsymbol h}).$$ The Dirichlet series $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$ converges absolutely for $\operatorname{Re}s>1$, while the series in <a href="#eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series">[eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series]</a> at $1-s$ converges absolutely for $\operatorname{Re}s<0$. The functional equation <a href="#eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation">[eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation]</a> and Stirling’s formula <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), 5.A.4] therefore give polynomial bounds in $|\operatorname{Im}s|$ for $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$ on both sides of the strip $0\le\operatorname{Re}s\le1$. Splitting the integral defining $\mathcal J(s)$ at $v=1$ gives finite order; Phragmén–Lindelöf <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[IK04](#ref-IK04), Theorem 5.53] then gives the same type of bound inside the strip (compare the arguments of Dunn and Radziwiłł in <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[DR](#ref-DR), Propositions 5.1–5.2]). Together with the rapid decay of $\widehat V_*$ on vertical lines, these bounds justify moving the $s$-contour in <a href="#eq:theta-mellin-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-mellin-inversion">[eq:theta-mellin-inversion]</a> to $\operatorname{Re}s<0$. No poles are crossed, since $\mathcal T(s,\Psi)$ is entire. Setting $t=\tfrac12-s$ then gives a line $\operatorname{Re}t>1/2$, where the dual coefficient series converges absolutely.

For fixed $h_0$ and active set, insert <a href="#eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series">[eq:dual-cusp-mellin-series]</a> into <a href="#eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation">[eq:theta-mellin-functional-equation]</a> and use <a href="#eq:ray-local-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-local-transform">[eq:ray-local-transform]</a> to sum over the nonzero $h_p$. After the normalization in <a href="#eq:ray-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-mellin">[eq:ray-mellin]</a>, the scalar $C$ in <a href="#eq:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reflection">[eq:reflection]</a> for this group of translates is $$C=-\frac{\mathrm i}{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}
 \widehat\phi(h_0)\overline{\kappa_0}
 \prod_{p\ {\rm inactive}}(1-\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)^{-1})
 \prod_{p\ {\rm active}}\chi_p(\sigma_p)^{-2}\omega_{p,j_p},
 \qquad |C|\le1/81 .$$ Dividing <a href="#eq:ray-mellin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-mellin">[eq:ray-mellin]</a> by its gamma factors and setting $t=\tfrac12-s$ produces the gamma quotient in <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a>. Its numerator gamma factors have their first pole at $t=-5/6$, and its reciprocal denominator gamma factors are entire. Thus no poles lie between the current contour $\operatorname{Re}t>1/2$ and $\operatorname{Re}t=0$. The rapid decay of $\widehat V_*$, together with Stirling’s formula, allows us to shift each kernel contour to $\operatorname{Re}t=0$. This gives the weight $V_*^\sharp$ defined in <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a>, evaluated at $\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\ell)X/\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(c)^2$. Together with <a href="#eq:ray-local-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-local-transform">[eq:ray-local-transform]</a>, this gives the dual sum <a href="#eq:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reflection">[eq:reflection]</a>.

For $k_0$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:reflection-uniformity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection-uniformity">15</a>, fix $h_0$, the factors of $\Psi$ other than $\chi_{k_0}$, and the active/inactive choices at their primes. Every prime dividing $k_0$ has exponent $j_p=1$ and is therefore active, so $$r=k_0\prod_{p\in\mathcal A_{\rm fix}}p.$$ Thus $r/k_0$ is fixed, and fixing $k_0\bmod M^2$ fixes $r\bmod M^2$. The choices of $H,c_0$ and the residues in <a href="#eq:ray-additive-crt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-additive-crt">[eq:ray-additive-crt]</a> are therefore fixed in each such class. Thus $d=d_\sigma$, $\psi$, and $c_0$ have precisely the asserted dependence on $k_0$.

<a id="eq:ray-kernel"></a>
It remains to bound the transformed weight. The first numerator pole of the gamma quotient in <a href="#eq:theta-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight">[eq:theta-weight]</a> is at $t=-5/6$. We may therefore shift the kernel contour to $\operatorname{Re}t=-1/4$ for $0<x\le1$, obtaining the factor $x^{1/4}$, and to $\operatorname{Re}t=A$ for $x\ge1$, obtaining $x^{-A}$. Each application of $x\partial_x$ introduces a factor $-t$. Stirling’s formula on $-1/4\le\operatorname{Re}t\le A$ then gives, for every $A>0$ and $j\ge0$, $$\label{eq:ray-kernel}
\begin{aligned}
|(x\partial_x)^jV_*^\sharp(x)|
 &\ll_{A,j}\min\{x^{1/4},(1+x)^{-A}\}\\
 &\quad\times\sup_{-A\le\eta\le1/4}\int_{\mathbb R}
 (1+|u|)^{\lceil4A\rceil+j+2}|\widehat V_*(\eta+\mathrm i u)|\,du.
\end{aligned}$$ When $V_*$ is supported in a fixed compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$, repeated integration by parts in its Mellin transform gives rapid decay in $|u|$, uniformly for $-A\le\eta\le1/4$. Thus, for a sufficiently large $J=J(A,j)$, the supremum of integrals in <a href="#eq:ray-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-kernel">[eq:ray-kernel]</a> is $\ll_{A,j,I}\|V_*\|_{C^J(I)}$. This proves <a href="#eq:theta-weight-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-weight-decay">[eq:theta-weight-decay]</a>. Thus <a href="#eq:reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reflection">[eq:reflection]</a> expresses $T(X;\Psi)$ as $O_{\Psi_0,S}(2^{|\mathcal P|})$ dual sums with $|C|\le1/81$, the asserted ray class dependence of $(d,\psi,c_0)$, and the weight decay just established. Together with the support and coefficient bounds proved above, this completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#lem:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection">14</a> and Lemmas <a href="#lem:reflection-uniformity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reflection-uniformity">15</a> and <a href="#lem:theta-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:theta-bounds">16</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:weights"></a>
### Separating variables in smooth weights

This appendix justifies the separation of smooth weights in the completed mean-square estimate of Section <a href="#sec:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reflection">6</a>, and the treatment of kernels depending on the row in the Poisson reductions of Sections <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">4</a> and <a href="#sec:transfer-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer-proof">7</a>.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Separating the variables

We use Mellin inversion to separate the variables of a smooth weight, with coefficient bounds controlled by finitely many derivatives.

<a id="stmt-lem-smooth"></a>
<div id="lem:smooth" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:smooth-separation"></a>
**Lemma 22**. *Fix a box $\mathcal I=I_1\times\cdots\times I_d$, where each $I_j$ is a compact interval in $(0,\infty)$. Every $\mathcal K\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty)^d)$ supported in $\mathcal I$ has a representation $$\label{eq:smooth-separation}
 \mathcal K(\mathbf x)=\int_{\mathbb R^d}b(\mathbf t)
       \prod_{j=1}^d x_j^{\mathrm i t_j}\,d\mathbf t,
 \qquad x_j>0.$$ For $J\ge0$ and every even integer $q>J+d$, the coefficient satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb R^d}|b(\mathbf t)|(1+|\mathbf t|)^J\,d\mathbf t
 \ll_{\mathcal I,J,q,d}\|\mathcal K\|_{C^q(\mathcal I)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smooth"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smooth</summary>

*Proof.* Take the Fourier transform in logarithmic coordinates: $$b(\mathbf t)=\frac1{(2\pi)^d}\int_{\mathbb R^d}
 \mathcal K(e^{y_1},\ldots,e^{y_d})
 e^{-\mathrm i\mathbf t\cdot\mathbf y}\,d\mathbf y.$$ Applying Fourier inversion to this gives <a href="#eq:smooth-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-separation">[eq:smooth-separation]</a>. Since the intervals $I_j$ are fixed and bounded away from zero, the $C^q$ norm in logarithmic coordinates is bounded by a constant times $\|\mathcal K\|_{C^q(\mathcal I)}$. Applying $(1-\Delta_{\mathbf y})^{q/2}$ under the integral therefore gives $$|b(\mathbf t)|\ll_{\mathcal I,q,d}
 (1+|\mathbf t|)^{-q}\|\mathcal K\|_{C^q(\mathcal I)}
 \qquad(q\ge0\text{ even}).$$ Multiplication by $(1+|\mathbf t|)^J$ and integration proves the bound when $q>J+d$. See also the multivariable Mellin formulation in <a id="pm-cite-83"></a>[[PY19](#ref-PY19), 10.1, (130)–(131)]. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:smooth-pointwise"></a>
In our applications, the weight has the more specific form $$\mathcal K_R(\mathbf x)=\prod_{j=1}^dW_j(x_j)
 F\Bigl(R\prod_{j=1}^d x_j^{a_j}\Bigr),\qquad R>0,$$ where the exponents $a_j$ are fixed real numbers and $W_j\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty))$ is supported in $I_j$. Assume that $F\in C^\infty((0,\infty))$ satisfies $$\|F\|_{A,q}:=\max_{0\le m\le q}\sup_{u>0}
 (1+u)^A\bigl|(u\partial_u)^mF(u)\bigr|<\infty
 \qquad(A\ge0,\ q\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}).$$ On the fixed box $\mathcal I$, the argument of $F$ is comparable to $R$. The chain rule and the pointwise bound in the proof give coefficients $b_R$ satisfying, for $A\ge0$ and even $q\ge0$, $$\label{eq:smooth-pointwise}
 |b_R(\mathbf t)|
 \ll (1+R)^{-A}(1+|\mathbf t|)^{-q}\|F\|_{A,q}
       \prod_{j=1}^d\|W_j\|_{C^q(I_j)}.$$ Consequently, for $J\ge0$ and even $q>J+d$, <a id="eq:smooth-weight-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:smooth-weight-bound}
 \int_{\mathbb R^d}|b_R(\mathbf t)|(1+|\mathbf t|)^J\,d\mathbf t
 \ll (1+R)^{-A}\|F\|_{A,q}
       \prod_{j=1}^d\|W_j\|_{C^q(I_j)}.$$ The constants depend only on $A,J,q,d$, the intervals $I_j$, and the exponents $a_j$. Thus separation preserves the decay in $R$, and its cost is controlled by finitely many derivatives of the original weights.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Weights depending on the row

The next lemma extends mean-square bounds for a common test function to smooth kernels depending on the row, with losses controlled by uniform bounds on their derivatives.

Fix compact intervals $I\subset\operatorname{int}I_*$ in $(0,\infty)$ and an integer $m\ge0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-smooth-mean-square"></a>
<div id="lem:smooth-mean-square" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:coupled-mean-square"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *Consider two families of finite sums $$S_{j,r}(U)=\sum_n a_{j,r}(n)U(x_{j,r,n}),\qquad
 j=1,2,\quad x_{j,r,n}>0,$$ with a finite set of rows $r$ and nonnegative weights $w_r$. Suppose that, for every $U\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$, $$\sum_r w_r|S_{j,r}(U)|^2\le M_j\|U\|_{C^m(I_*)}^2,
 \qquad j=1,2.$$ Then for any $|c_r|\le w_r$ and smooth kernels $\mathcal K_r$ supported in $I^2$, $$\label{eq:coupled-mean-square}
 \Bigl|\sum_r c_r\sum_{n_1,n_2}
 a_{1,r}(n_1)\overline{a_{2,r}(n_2)}
 \mathcal K_r(x_{1,r,n_1},x_{2,r,n_2})\Bigr|
 \ll_{I,I_*,m}\sqrt{M_1M_2}\,
       \sup_r\|\mathcal K_r\|_{C^{2m+4}(I^2)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smooth-mean-square"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smooth-mean-square</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a real $V\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$ equal to one on $I$, and set $U_t(x)=V(x)x^{\mathrm i t}$. Apply <a href="#eq:smooth-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-separation">[eq:smooth-separation]</a> of Lemma <a href="#lem:smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth">22</a> to each $\mathcal K_r$ with $d=2$ and $\mathcal I=I^2$. Replacing the second Mellin variable by its negative and multiplying by $V(x)V(y)$, which equals one on the support of $\mathcal K_r$, gives $$\mathcal K_r(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb R^2}b_r(s,t)
 U_s(x)\overline{U_t(y)}\,ds\,dt,$$ with the following uniform bound, obtained from the pointwise coefficient estimate in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth">22</a> with $d=2$ and $\mathcal I=I^2$: $$\sup_r|b_r(s,t)|\ll_{I,q}
 \frac{\sup_r\|\mathcal K_r\|_{C^q(I^2)}}{(1+|s|+|t|)^q}
 \qquad(q\ge0\text{ even}).$$ The hypothesis applies to each $U_t$, and $\|U_t\|_{C^m(I_*)}\ll_{I,I_*,m}(1+|t|)^m$. Taking the common bound for $b_r$ before integrating and applying Cauchy–Schwarz in $r$ therefore bounds the absolute value in <a href="#eq:coupled-mean-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coupled-mean-square">[eq:coupled-mean-square]</a> by a constant depending on $I,I_*,m,q$ times <a id="eq:kernel-bilinear-integral"></a>$$\label{eq:kernel-bilinear-integral}
 \sqrt{M_1M_2}\sup_r\|\mathcal K_r\|_{C^q(I^2)}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}
 \frac{(1+|s|)^m(1+|t|)^m}{(1+|s|+|t|)^q}\,ds\,dt.$$ Taking $q=2m+4$ makes the integral converge and proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Recombining the separated sums

We use weighted Cauchy–Schwarz to pass from mean-square bounds for the separated sums to a bound for their weighted integral.

<a id="stmt-lem-recombine"></a>
<div id="lem:recombine" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:integral-mean-square"></a>
**Lemma 24**. *Let $r,\iota$ range over finite sets, let $d\ge1$, and let $w_r\ge0$. Suppose the measurable coefficients $c_{\iota,r}$ satisfy $$|c_{\iota,r}(\mathbf t)|\le b_\iota(\mathbf t),\qquad
 M:=\sum_\iota\int_{\mathbb R^d}b_\iota(\mathbf t)\,d\mathbf t<\infty,$$ where $b_\iota:\mathbb R^d\to[0,\infty)$. For measurable $F_{\iota,\mathbf t}(r)$, whenever the right-hand side is finite, one has $$\label{eq:integral-mean-square}
 \sum_r w_r\Bigl|\sum_\iota\int c_{\iota,r}(\mathbf t)
 F_{\iota,\mathbf t}(r)\,d\mathbf t\Bigr|^2
 \le M\sum_\iota\int b_\iota(\mathbf t)
       \sum_r w_r|F_{\iota,\mathbf t}(r)|^2\,d\mathbf t.$$ The same assertion holds for finite sums with the integrals omitted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-recombine"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-recombine</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed $r$, weighted Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\Bigl|\sum_\iota\int c_{\iota,r}(\mathbf t)
 F_{\iota,\mathbf t}(r)\,d\mathbf t\Bigr|^2
 \le M\sum_\iota\int b_\iota(\mathbf t)
 |F_{\iota,\mathbf t}(r)|^2\,d\mathbf t.$$ Multiply by $w_r$ and sum over $r$ to obtain <a href="#eq:integral-mean-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:integral-mean-square">[eq:integral-mean-square]</a>. The same argument with sums in place of integrals proves the finite version. ◻

</details>

[^1]: For a primary prime $p\nmid6$ and $p\nmid u$, $(u/p)_6$ is the unique sixth root of unity congruent to $u^{(\mathrm N_{K/\mathbb Q}(p)-1)/6}$ modulo $p$; set $(u/p)_6=0$ when $p\mid u$. Extend multiplicatively in the denominator to define $\chi_n(u)=(u/n)_6$ for primary $n$ coprime to $6$. For the unit ideal, $\chi_1(u)=1$ for every $u$, including $u=0$.

[^2]: For this terminology, see , , and .

[^3]: The ray class group modulo an ideal $\mathfrak m$ is the group of fractional ideals prime to $\mathfrak m$, modulo principal ideals generated by elements congruent to $1$ modulo $\mathfrak m$. A ray class character is a character of this finite group. For an element prime to $\mathfrak m$, its ray class means the class of its principal ideal.
